> Legal Context - Enforceability of Online Terms
> Courts consistently hold that online terms are not enforceable where a consumer is not provided reasonably conspicuous notice of the terms and does not provide a clear and unambiguous manifestation of assent.
> Core Legal Principle
> Courts begin with a basic rule of contract law: a contract requires mutual assent (agreement).
> If a user did not receive reasonable notice of the terms and did not clearly and unambiguously manifest assent to those terms, courts find that no contract was formed at all.
> Plain meaning:
> A company cannot enforce hidden terms or policies if the customer was never clearly shown those terms and never clearly agreed to them.
> If the user never agreed to the Terms of Use, none of the provisions within those terms are binding.
> These decisions reinforce the established rule that browsewrap-style terms presented without clear notice and affirmative acceptance do not create enforceable agreements.
> ________________
> 
> 
> Key Court Decisions
> Nguyen v. Barnes & Noble Inc.
> 763 F.3d 1171 (9th Cir. 2014)
> The Ninth Circuit affirmed denial of arbitration, holding that a browsewrap hyperlink alone - even when visible near checkout buttons - is insufficient to bind a user absent actual notice or an unambiguous manifestation of assent.
> The court ruled that a hyperlink to “Terms of Use” on a website is not enough to bind a user when the user is not required to click agreement or otherwise clearly indicate acceptance of the terms.
> Plain meaning:
> Simply placing terms somewhere online on a website does not automatically make them binding if the customer never clearly and knowingly agreed to them.
> ________________
> 
> 
> Specht v. Netscape Communications Corp.
> 150 F. Supp. 2d 585 (S.D.N.Y. 2001)
> The court held that downloading software without being required to affirmatively click “I agree” does not constitute assent to hidden or inconspicuous license terms, and therefore arbitration could not be compelled.
> The court specifically held that downloading software alone is not an unambiguous manifestation of assent, particularly where the license terms were not clearly presented to the user.
> Plain meaning:
> A company cannot claim you agreed to hidden terms simply because you used or downloaded something.
> ________________
> 
> 
> Berman v. Freedom Financial Network, LLC
> More recently, the Ninth Circuit reaffirmed that consumers cannot be bound by contractual or arbitration provisions embedded in website terms where the interface fails to clearly inform users that they are agreeing to those terms and fails to obtain an unambiguous manifestation of assent.
> The court emphasized that website terms are enforceable only when users receive clear notice of the terms and provide unambiguous agreement to them.
> Where terms are hidden, buried in hyperlinks, or never clearly accepted by the user, courts find that no binding agreement exists.
> ________________
> 
> 
> Required Elements for Enforceable Website Terms
> Courts consistently require both of the following:
> 1. Reasonably conspicuous notice of the terms, and
> 2. An unambiguous manifestation of assent by the user
> If either element is missing, courts generally refuse enforcement.
> If a website does not provide reasonably conspicuous notice of the terms and obtain the user’s clear and unambiguous agreement, courts generally find that no contract was formed and the terms are not enforceable.
> Hidden terms, buried hyperlinks, or terms that a user never clearly and affirmatively agreed to are therefore not binding.
> Courts routinely refuse to enforce website terms where the interface design fails to provide sufficiently conspicuous notice and fails to obtain clear and unambiguous user assent.
> ________________
> 
> 
> U.S. Supreme Court Principle
> These rulings reflect a foundational contract principle recognized by the U.S. Supreme Court: arbitration is a matter of consent and cannot be imposed absent a valid agreement.
> See AT&T Technologies, Inc. v. Communications Workers of America:
> “Arbitration is a matter of contract and a party cannot be required to submit to arbitration any dispute which he has not agreed so to submit.”
> Plain meaning:
> A company cannot force someone into contract terms they never clearly and knowingly agreed to.
> ________________
> 
> 
> What This Means for Online Terms
> If a court finds that a user never clearly and unambiguously agreed to the Terms of Use, the entire set of terms may be unenforceable, including, but not limited to:
> * deliverables
> * arbitration clauses
> * liability limitations
> * class action waivers
> * merchant “terms and conditions” or policy provisions
> Plain meaning:
> If the customer never clearly agreed to the terms, the company cannot rely on those terms to limit responsibility or enforce policies.
> ________________
> 
> 
> Bottom Line
> Courts repeatedly reject attempts by companies to enforce website terms or policies when:
> * the terms were hidden or difficult to notice, and
> * the consumer never provided clear and unambiguous agreement to those terms
> Simple takeaway:
> Hidden terms or “you agreed by using the website” policies often do not hold up in court without clear notice and unambiguous consent.
> ________________
> 
> 
> Judicial Standard Applied by Courts
> Courts evaluating online terms repeatedly emphasize that a user must be placed on clear notice of the terms and must provide an unambiguous manifestation of assent before those terms become binding. As the Second Circuit explained in Specht v. Netscape Communications Corp.:
> “A reasonably prudent Internet user would not have known or learned of the existence of the license terms … and therefore did not unambiguously manifest assent to those terms.”
> This principle is repeatedly applied in cases such as Nguyen v. Barnes & Noble Inc. and Berman v. Freedom Financial Network, LLC, where courts refused to enforce website terms because users were not given clear notice and did not clearly agree to them.
> Plain meaning:
> If a person online does not clearly understand that they are agreeing to specific terms, and are proven to have agreed to those specific terms, courts generally find that no binding agreement exists.
> If a person online is not unambiguously made aware of clearly specific terms, and clearly, indisputably consents to & agrees with those specific terms, courts find that no binding agreement exists.
> 
> 
> 
> 
> ________________
> 
> 
> 
> 
> CONCLUSION / SUMMARY
> Courts do not ask whether a merchant technically displayed terms somewhere on a website. Instead, they examine whether a reasonably prudent user was placed on reasonably conspicuous notice of specific terms and clearly and unambiguously manifested assent to those terms.
> If a user is not clearly made aware of specific terms from the user’s perspective and experience, and does not clearly and unambiguously agree to those terms, courts generally find that no binding agreement exists - regardless of whether the merchant claims the terms were technically displayed, delivered, or merely available on the website.
> Availability of terms alone does not create assent; clear notice and unambiguous agreement are required for a binding contract to exist.

## 🤖 ChatGPT

Below are the **actual legal frameworks that govern banks when handling chargebacks and billing-error disputes.**  
These are the statutes and regulations that **force banks to investigate properly and not simply accept a merchant’s claim.**

I will cite the controlling laws and the **exact obligations imposed on banks.**

---

# 1. The Primary Law: Fair Credit Billing Act (FCBA)

**Statute:**  
15 U.S.C. §1666

The FCBA governs credit-card billing disputes and chargebacks.

It requires card issuers to conduct a **reasonable investigation** when a consumer disputes a charge.

### Key rule
A bank **cannot simply rely on the merchant’s statement**.

**Statutory language**

15 U.S.C. §1666(a):

> “If a creditor… receives a written notice from the obligor… the creditor shall investigate the matter and correct the billing error…”

Source  
https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/15/1666

---

# 2. Regulation Z (FCBA Implementation Rule)

**Regulation:**  
12 CFR §1026.13

This is the **federal rule banks must follow during disputes.**

### Investigation requirement

12 CFR §1026.13(f)

> “The creditor must conduct a reasonable investigation of the consumer’s claim.”

Source  
https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1026/13/

A bank violates federal law if it:

• ignores evidence from the consumer  
• blindly accepts merchant claims  
• fails to investigate conflicting evidence

---

# 3. CFPB Official Interpretation (Binding Guidance)

The Consumer Financial Protection Bureau clarified that **issuers cannot simply accept the merchant’s explanation**.

Official Interpretation §1026.13(f):

> “The creditor may not deny a claim solely because the merchant asserts that the charge is valid.”

Source  
https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1026/interp-13/

This is critical.

It means:

A bank **must evaluate evidence independently**.

---

# 4. Error Type Covered by Law

Regulation Z defines billing errors including:

12 CFR §1026.13(a)(3)

> “A reflection on a statement of goods or services not accepted by the consumer or not delivered as agreed.”

Source  
https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1026/13/#a-3

This includes:

• non-delivery  
• misrepresentation  
• bait-and-switch  
• failure to provide promised services

---

# 5. Bank Liability if They Ignore Evidence

Failure to comply with FCBA creates statutory liability.

15 U.S.C. §1640

Provides damages for violations.

Consumer may recover:

• actual damages  
• statutory damages  
• attorney fees

Source  
https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/15/1640

---

# 6. Unfair Practices by Banks

Banks can also violate the **Consumer Financial Protection Act (CFPA)**.

12 U.S.C. §5531

This prohibits **unfair or abusive acts in financial services**.

CFPB definition of unfair act:

> An act is unfair when it causes substantial injury to consumers that is not reasonably avoidable.

Source  
https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/12/5531

If a bank:

• knowingly supports fraudulent merchant claims  
• denies disputes without investigation  
• relies on fabricated agreements

This may constitute a **UDAAP violation**.

---

# 7. Electronic Fund Transfer Act (for debit cards)

If debit was used, the governing law is:

15 U.S.C. §1693f

Requires investigation of unauthorized transfers.

Source  
https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/15/1693f

---

# 8. Card Network Rules (Visa / Mastercard)

While not statutes, banks **contractually must follow them.**

Example (Visa Core Rules):

Issuers must ensure disputes are supported by evidence and properly reviewed.

Source  
https://usa.visa.com/support/consumer/visa-rules.html

If a bank ignores evidence, they may violate network rules.

---

# 9. Merchant Contract Enforceability (Important for your case)

If the merchant’s “agreement” was never accepted, **the charge may not be valid.**

Courts consistently hold that online terms require:

• conspicuous notice  
• clear assent

If these are missing, **no contract exists**. fileciteturn0file0

This principle appears in:

• **Specht v. Netscape**  
• **Nguyen v. Barnes & Noble**  
• **Berman v. Freedom Financial**

All holding that **hidden terms are not binding**. fileciteturn0file0

---

# 10. When a Bank Becomes Legally Exposed

A bank may face liability if it:

1. ignores evidence
2. relies solely on merchant claims
3. fails to conduct reasonable investigation
4. denies a billing error without explanation

These violate:

• FCBA  
• Regulation Z  
• CFPA / UDAAP

---

# 11. Evidence That Shows Bank Non-Compliance

Regulators typically look for:

• failure to review submitted evidence  
• missing case logs  
• unexplained dispute denial  
• merchant documents accepted without verification

This is why requesting the **full representment packet and internal logs** is powerful.

---

# Bottom Line

The main laws protecting you are:

| Law | What it requires |
|---|---|
FCBA (15 USC 1666) | Bank must investigate billing errors |
Regulation Z (12 CFR 1026.13) | Requires reasonable investigation |
CFPA (12 USC 5531) | Prohibits unfair banking practices |
TILA damages (15 USC 1640) | Allows consumer lawsuits |
EFTA (15 USC 1693f) | Debit card dispute protections |

A bank **cannot simply side with a merchant** without a real investigation.

---

**The specific CFPB language that proves banks cannot rely solely on merchant claims.**

That rule alone wins a large number of disputes.

# CFPB Complaint – Billing Error Investigation Failure

**Company:** First Internet Bank (FIB)  
**Product:** Credit Card  
**Issue Type:** Billing Error / Dispute Investigation Failure

---

## Complaint

I submitted a billing-error dispute regarding a $10,500 transaction for a “done-for-you Airbnb service” that was never delivered as promised.

The merchant later produced a document claiming the existence of a binding agreement containing terms that were never presented, acknowledged, or accepted at the time of purchase.

Evidence submitted to the bank demonstrates:

• No signed agreement  
• No acknowledgment of any terms or conditions  
• Written messages from the merchant admitting that no agreement was provided at the time of purchase  
• Marketing materials and invoices contradicting the merchant’s later claims

Despite this evidence, First Internet Bank denied the billing-error dispute.

The bank appears to have relied solely on the merchant’s unsupported assertions without independently evaluating the conflicting evidence provided by the consumer.

This raises serious concerns regarding compliance with the Fair Credit Billing Act and Regulation Z.

---

## Applicable Law

Under **15 U.S.C. §1666 (Fair Credit Billing Act)** and **12 CFR §1026.13**, creditors must conduct a **reasonable investigation** when a billing error is disputed.

Regulation Z defines billing errors to include charges for:

> “goods or services not accepted by the consumer or not delivered as agreed.”  
12 CFR §1026.13(a)(3)

Additionally, CFPB commentary states that creditors may not deny a claim solely because a merchant asserts the charge is valid.

---

## Compliance Concerns

Based on the bank’s actions, the following issues appear present:

1. Failure to conduct a reasonable investigation as required by Regulation Z.

2. Reliance on merchant representations despite conflicting evidence provided by the consumer.

3. Failure to evaluate whether the merchant’s alleged agreement was ever actually presented or accepted.

4. Failure to address documented evidence that the services promised were not delivered as agreed.

---

## Documentation Requested

To independently verify compliance with federal law, I request that the bank provide:

1. The complete merchant representment packet received from the merchant.
2. All attachments relied upon in the dispute determination.
3. The internal dispute investigation log and timeline.
4. Documentation showing how the bank evaluated the conflicting evidence provided by the consumer.
5. The exact date the merchant rebuttal was received.

---

## Requested Resolution

I request that the bank:

• Reopen the billing-error investigation  
• Conduct a compliant and independent investigation under Regulation Z  
• Reverse the disputed charge if the merchant cannot substantiate the claim with credible evidence

---

## Supporting Evidence

Supporting documentation includes:

• written communications with the merchant  
• marketing materials and invoices  
• screenshots and written statements from the merchant  
• documentation showing the terms were never presented or accepted

These materials demonstrate that the merchant’s later claims contradict the actual purchase process and representations made at the time of sale.

---

# Why This Version Works

CFPB complaints work best when they:

• focus on **bank compliance failure**  
• cite **specific law**  
• request **specific documents**  
• avoid emotional language

This version does exactly that.

---

If you'd like, I can also show you **three powerful sentences you can add that dramatically increase the odds CFPB forces the bank to reopen the investigation.**

> Compliance Department
> PO Box 30918

> 11150 Rhode Island Ave S

> Re: Consumer Financial Protection Bureau Case 260217-28952925 & 260219-29048782
> Dear Mr. Nguyen,
> We recently received your newest complaints submitted to the Consumer Financial Protection 
> Bureau and have conducted additional research regarding your claims.
> As previously communicated to you, records indicate that all applicable transaction dispute 
> policies and procedures were properly followed. We have found no evidence of discrimination 
> or regulatory violations. Receipt of your dispute was acknowledged, research was conducted, 
> and a decision was communicated to you all within allowable timeframes.
> Our Credit Card Fraud Operations Team recently re-reviewed your claim, D2522303892, in 
> detail. Records indicate you were sent a ‘Need More Information Letter’ on August 20, 2025,
> which you responded to, and the response was attached to the claim on September 5, 2025. 
> Utilizing the information you provided, we attempted to dispute the transaction with the 
> merchant. The merchant responded to our dispute on October 30, 2025. Based on the 
> response there were additional items we needed you to address. Another ‘Need More 
> Information Letter’ was mailed on November 14, 2025, to the same address as the previous 
> letters. You did not respond to the November 14th letter on or prior to the established 
> deadline.
> I have spoken with our Credit Card Fraud Operations Team who advised me that First 
> Interstate has no record of a travel notice on your account or any notes indicating that you 
> were travelling during the timeframe in which dispute communication was sent to you by the 
> Bank. Regardless, records indicate you communicated to our Fraud Team that everything we 
> needed according to the second ‘Need More Information Letter’ was provided in your 
> response to the first letter.
> You raised issue with the reason for the chargeback being that the purchase was non￾refundable. Although you claim non-delivery, the merchant presented evidence to First 
> Interstate Bank that they provided 129.5 service hours, provided you with 93 professionally 
> underwritten properties in your market of choice, and purchased AirDNA PPRO Membership 
> and AirBitrage.ai property marketplace access for you in accordance with your agreement.
> 
> <PARSED TEXT FOR PAGE: 4 / 45>
> 
> Lastly, you claimed that the merchant cannot on one hand provide a refund and on the other 
> state the service is non-refundable. The merchant’s notes and text messages between the 
> merchant and you state that the refund was a result of a downgrade in the services from the 
> Enterprise Package to the Pro Package (2 properties to 1 property). After the refund, you 
> continued to work with the company, participating in consulting sessions for 45 days.
> I apologize for any frustration you may have experienced, and I am sorry that First Interstate 
> is unable to further assist you with this matter. We encourage you to continue to work 
> directly with the merchant to resolve your issue.
> Sincerely,
> Jo

> We received your complaint. Thank you.
> We will review your complaint. Depending on what we find, we will typically:
> Send your complaint to the company for a response; or
> Send your complaint to another state or federal agency, or help you get in touch with your state or local consumer protection
> office; or
> Let you know if we need more information to continue our work.
> YOUR COMPLAINT
> Regulation Z & UDAAP Noncompliance - Failure to Investigate and Ongoing Violations This complaint concerns
> First Interstate Bank’s failure to conduct a reasonable investigation, and its willful misconduct, willful negligence,
> reckless disregard, and willful noncompliance with Regulation Z (12 CFR §1026.13) and Dodd-Frank UDAAP
> standards in handling my credit card billing dispute. This submission is not a duplicate of CFPB Case 260131-
> 28391819. That complaint addressed the underlying transaction. This complaint addresses the bank’s procedural and
> substantive violations during and after the dispute process. First Interstate Bank denied a services-not-rendered dispute
> without identifying any evidence that the contracted service was delivered. No property was delivered, no listing
> launched, no revenue generated, and the merchant issued a partial refund acknowledging non-delivery. Despite this
> undisputed record, the bank denied the dispute and continues to charge interest. The bank relied on unsigned, post￾payment merchant terms, refused to provide the merchant’s rebuttal documentation, and refused to disclose the dates
> the rebuttal was received, processed, or reviewed, preventing verification of compliance with Regulation Z. The bank
> gave the merchant approximately 60-90+ days to respond (Aug 13, 2025 to Nov 13, 2025), while giving me fewer
> than 10 days, by physical mail only, despite knowing I was traveling and after I requested digital delivery. I have
> never received the alleged November 14, 2025 letter, yet the dispute was closed anyway. The bank now claims it
> “cannot assist” because internal timelines have passed. This is legally false. Regulation Z provides no safe harbor for
> denying a billing error based on procedure when the investigation is unreasonable or when the underlying facts
> establish non-delivery. A procedurally timely denial that ratifies merchant non-performance is not compliance - it is
> facilitation. Temporary access to: AirDNA, marketplaces, or consultation does not constitute delivery of the promised
> DFY merchant services with an abundance of proof provided on the subject in detail, directly from the merchant
> themselves. Under Mastercard Reason Code 4853, the test is binary: Was the promised service delivered? Here, the
> answer is no. The promised service with provided proof explicitly states a DFY (Done-For-You) Airbnb acquired
> property; designed, furnished, & listed that profits $3,500-$5,000+/mo after operational expenses. Token or
> preparatory activity cannot cure total non-delivery. No timeline argument can cure that defect. First Interstate Bank
> continues to demonstrate willful negligence, and routinely ignores action relating to directly addressed compliance
> with UDAAP, REG Z, proper investigation, etc. They refuse to answer compliance related questions, and continue to
> violate consumer chargeback rights, and are abusing their powers as a bank, due to a lack of due diligence, and
> repeated documented willful misconduct. I am requesting immediate supervisory review and administrative
> correction. These Regulation Z and UDAAP violations persist after notice and appeal, warranting CFPB oversight.
> This must be escalated to all relevant enforcement agencies.
> ATTACHMENTS

> <PARSED TEXT FOR PAGE: 1 / 306>
> 
> Regulation Z Compliance Failure - Issuer
> Denied Billing Error While Refusing to
> Produce Evidence Relied Upon
> Attached is the details of what happened, in addition to a large packet of documented
> evidence, proofs, and communication history with First Interstate Bank (FIB)
> Summary
> This complaint concerns First Interstate Bank’s refusal to produce the investigation
> documentation it claimed to have relied upon in denying a billing-error dispute, despite
> repeated documented requests over an approximately seven-month period.
> The issuer represented that merchant documentation and internal records were used to support
> its determination. However, despite requests submitted through certified mail, fax
> transmissions, and recorded telephone calls, the bank refused or failed to produce the
> materials it claimed to have relied upon.
> The requested investigation documentation was only produced after intervention by the
> Division of Banking and Financial Institutions (DBFI).
> The prolonged refusal to produce the materials relied upon in the dispute determination raises a
> separate regulatory concern regarding the issuer’s dispute documentation practices,
> record retention, and whether the investigation referenced in prior communications with
> regulators was supported by the underlying records at the time the determination was
> made.
> Core Regulatory Issue
> The central issue is issuer compliance with federal billing-error investigation
> requirements, not simply the underlying merchant dispute.
> 
> <PARSED TEXT FOR PAGE: 2 / 306>
> 
> Under Regulation Z (12 CFR §1026.13), issuers must:
> ● conduct a reasonable investigation
> ● determine whether a billing error occurred
> ● explain the determination
> ● provide documentation relied upon when requested
> If the issuer cannot produce the documents it relied upon, the determination cannot be
> independently verified as compliant with Regulation Z.
> Undisputed Timeline
> Aug 13, 2025
> Billing dispute opened.
> Sept 5, 2025
> Complete dispute documentation submitted.
> Sept 25, 2025
> Provisional credit issued.
> Oct 30, 2025
> Issuer states merchant provided rebuttal documentation.
> Nov 14, 2025
> Issuer claims a “Need More Information” letter was mailed.
> Nov 24, 2025
> Issuer claims dispute closed for non-response.
> However:
> ● I never received the alleged November 14 letter
> ● the issuer cannot provide proof of mailing or delivery
> ● the issuer refuses to provide the merchant documentation it relied upon
> Regulatory Deficiencies
> 
> <PARSED TEXT FOR PAGE: 3 / 306>
> 
> 1. Refusal to Produce Documentation Relied Upon
> The issuer states that merchant documentation was used to deny the dispute.
> Despite repeated written requests, the issuer has not produced:
> ● the complete merchant representment packet
> ● attachments referenced in the denial
> ● the dispute case log with timestamps
> ● the internal review documentation supporting the decision
> Without production of these materials, the reasonableness of the investigation required under
> 12 CFR §1026.13 cannot be independently verified.
> An investigation that cannot be verified based on documentary evidence raises a material
> compliance concern.
> Consumer Financial Protection Act (CFPA) Compliance
> Concerns
> The conduct described above raises potential compliance concerns under the Consumer
> Financial Protection Act’s prohibition on unfair, deceptive, or abusive acts or practices
> (UDAAP).
> ● 12 USC 5531 (UDAAP)
> ● 12 USC 5565 (civil penalties)
> Consumer Financial Protection Act
> Under the statute:
> “The Bureau may take any action… to prevent a covered person from committing or
> engaging in an unfair, deceptive, or abusive act or practice.”
> Source
> https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/12/5531
> The prolonged refusal to provide investigation documentation relied upon in a dispute
> determination, despite repeated documented requests and subsequent regulatory intervention,
> 
> <PARSED TEXT FOR PAGE: 4 / 306>
> 
> raises concerns regarding whether the issuer’s dispute-handling practices could result in
> avoidable consumer harm or procedural unfairness.
> Potential Regulatory Enforcement Authority
> If supervisory review identifies violations of the Consumer Financial Protection Act, the Bureau
> has authority to pursue civil penalties under:
> Consumer Financial Protection Act
> Civil penalty tiers authorized by statute include:
> • Up to $5,000 per day for ordinary violations
> • Up to $25,000 per day for reckless violations
> • Up to $1,000,000 per day for knowing violations
> Source
> https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/12/5565
> These penalties apply when regulators determine that covered financial institutions have
> engaged in conduct that violates federal consumer financial law.
> Additional Procedural Deficiencies and
> Notice Issues
> The issuer’s failure to produce the documentation relied upon occurred despite repeated
> documented requests over an extended period of time.
> Specifically, the requested investigation materials were sought through multiple channels,
> including:
> • Certified mail correspondence
> • Fax transmissions
> • Recorded telephone calls with bank representatives
> 
> <PARSED TEXT FOR PAGE: 5 / 306>
> 
> Despite these repeated documented requests, the issuer refused or failed to produce the
> requested materials for approximately seven months following the initial dispute filed in
> August 2025.
> The requested documentation was only produced after intervention by the state regulator,
> the Division of Banking and Financial Institutions (DBFI).
> Prior to that regulatory involvement, the issuer ignored or declined multiple consumer requests
> for the materials it claims to have relied upon in denying the billing error.
> This extended refusal to produce investigation documentation further prevents independent
> verification that a reasonable investigation occurred as required under Regulation Z (12 CFR
> §1026.13).
> The fact that the documentation was produced only after regulatory intervention further
> raises questions regarding the issuer’s repeated patterns of dispute-handling procedures
> and recordkeeping practices.
> Notice and Opportunity to Respond
> The issuer also closed the dispute for “non-response” based on an alleged November 14, 2025
> letter, which the issuer claims was mailed.
> However:
> • I never received the alleged letter
> • the issuer has not produced proof of mailing received or delivery confirmed to be
> received by client.
> • the issuer provided no alternative form of notice
> The issuer also denied any digital documentation to be provided upon, as well as
> sending the documentation to an alternative address, upon multiple requests.
> This issue is particularly concerning because the issuer had actual knowledge that I was
> traveling at the time.
> While traveling, the bank placed multiple fraud locks on my credit card, requiring me to
> contact the bank repeatedly to restore access to the account.
> During these calls:
> 
> <PARSED TEXT FOR PAGE: 6 / 306>
> 
> • I spoke with live bank representatives on recorded lines
> • I was required to explain multiple times that I was traveling
> • I provided the specific states I was traveling to in order to lift the fraud restrictions.
> Accordingly, the issuer had direct knowledge that I was traveling and had recently
> communicated with the bank through recorded calls.
> Despite that knowledge, the issuer:
> • relied solely on a single physical mail notice
> • provided fewer than approximately 10 days to respond
> • closed the dispute for non-response without proof that the notice was delivered
> Closing a billing-error dispute under these circumstances raises serious concerns regarding
> whether the consumer was given a meaningful opportunity to respond.
> 2. Closure of Dispute Without Proof of Notice
> The issuer claims a “Need More Information” letter was mailed on November 14, 2025.
> However, the issuer has not produced:
> ● proof of delivery.
> ● tracking number
> ● certified mail receipt

> Preparatory activity or consultation does not constitute delivery of the contracted service.
> Core Issue: Disputed Merchant Terms Used to Deny the
> Billing Error
> During the dispute investigation, the issuer relied on a document the merchant submitted as a
> “terms and agreement.” I did not receive, sign, acknowledge, or otherwise consent to that
> document at the time of purchase.
> The merchant’s own written communications indicate that no agreement was provided at the
> time of the transaction. In text messages sent more than one month after the purchase, the
> merchant states that an agreement had been omitted when the service was sold.
> Despite this, the issuer relied on the merchant-supplied document to conclude that the
> transaction was valid and that refunds were restricted. The merchant also attempted to change
> the scope of the transaction after payment by:
> ● reducing the promised service from two properties to one after missed timelines and
> non-delivery
> ● asserting a “no refund” position based on the disputed document
> ● claiming billable hours that were never agreed to in writing or acknowledged by the
> consumer.
> 
> <PARSED TEXT FOR PAGE: 8 / 306>
> 
> These assertions conflict with the merchant’s marketing materials, website representations,
> invoice, and written communications describing the service that was originally promised.
> Because the issuer relied on a document that was not agreed to by the consumer and that
> conflicts with the merchant’s own statements and promotional materials, the determination that
> no billing error occurred remains disputed.
> Supporting documentation, communications, marketing materials, and the merchant’s written
> statements are included in the attached evidence packet.
> The issuer has routinely failed to conduct a reasonable investigation as federal law requires.
> Systematically and repeatedly denied multiple appeals from the consumer, ignoring all evidence
> and denying the claim without performing their obligated duties of such. Including giving the
> consumer a meaningful opportunity to respond. All documentation and proof provided.
> Supervisory Escalation Concern
> The issuer has represented to the CFPB that it relied upon specific documentation in denying
> the billing error.
> However, despite repeated requests, the issuer has not produced the documentation it
> claims to have relied upon, including the merchant representment packet, internal dispute
> case log, or proof of mailing of the alleged November 14 notice.
> If the issuer cannot produce the documentation underlying its determination, the denial
> cannot be independently verified as compliant with Regulation Z (12 CFR §1026.13).
> This raises a material question regarding:
> ● the adequacy of the issuer’s billing-error investigation procedures
> ● its dispute documentation practices
> ● whether the representations made to the CFPB are supported by underlying records
> For this reason, I respectfully request supervisory review of the issuer’s Regulation Z
> dispute-handling and documentation practices in connection with this matter.

> To correct the billing error and restore the account to the position it would have been in absent

> [L3] ● avoids the delivery question
> [L4] ● enforces unsigned terms
> [L5] ● withholds required dates
> [L6] ● relies on non-receipt mail
> [L7] ● substitutes narrative for proof
> [L8] This is exactly the conduct Regulation Z and UDAAP exist to prevent.
> [L9] compress this into a 2–3 sentence CFPB “killer paragraph”, or
> [L10] convert it into Yes/No admissions that corner the bank legally.This complaint concerns First Interstate Bank’s violations of Regulation Z (12 CFR §1026.13)
> [L11] and Dodd-Frank UDAAP standards in its handling of my credit card billing dispute.
> [L12] This submission is not a duplicate of CFPB Case 260131-28391819.
> [L13] That prior complaint addressed the underlying merchant transaction.
> [L14] This complaint addresses First Interstate Bank’s unlawful dispute handling, procedural
> [L15] misconduct, and continued noncompliance after notice.
> [L16] First Interstate Bank denied a services-not-rendered dispute without identifying any evidence
> [L17] that the contracted service was delivered. No property was delivered, no Airbnb listing
> [L18] launched, no revenue generated, and the merchant issued a partial refund acknowledging
> [L19] non-delivery. Despite this undisputed record, the bank denied the dispute and continues to
> [L20] charge interest.
> [L21] The bank relied on unsigned, post-payment merchant terms, refused to provide the
> [L22] merchant’s rebuttal documentation, and refused to disclose the dates the rebuttal was received,
> [L23] processed, or reviewed. Without these records, neither the consumer nor the CFPB can verify
> [L24] compliance with Regulation Z’s reasonable investigation requirements.
> [L25] The bank allowed the merchant approximately 60-90+ days to respond (Aug 13, 2025 to Nov
> [L26] 13, 2025), while giving me fewer than 10 days to respond, exclusively by physical mail, during
> [L27] a period when the bank knew I was traveling and after I requested digital delivery multiple times.
> [L28] The bank alleges a letter dated Nov 14, 2025 and closed the dispute for non-response by Nov
> [L29] 24, 2025. I have never received the alleged letters, yet the dispute was closed anyway.
> [L30] Timeline compliance does not excuse this conduct. Regulation Z requires a reasonable
> [L31] investigation and an evidence-based determination. There is no safe harbor allowing a
> [L32] bank to deny a billing error dispute based on procedure when the underlying facts establish
> [L33] non-delivery. A procedurally timely denial that ratifies merchant non-performance is not
> [L34] compliance - it is facilitation.
> [L35] The core defect is outcome logic. First Interstate Bank denied a services-not-rendered dispute
> [L36] without any evidence of service delivery. No timeline argument can cure that defect.
> [L37] I am requesting immediate supervisory review and administrative correction. These
> [L38] Regulation Z and UDAAP violations persist after notice and appeal, warranting CFPB
> [L39] oversight.No Safe Harbor for Timeline Excuses (Regulation Z & UDAAP)
> [L40] First Interstate Bank continues to assert that it “cannot assist” because the matter is allegedly
> [L41] outside internal dispute or chargeback timeframes. This position is legally incorrect.
> [L42] There is no statute, regulation, or card-network rule that permits a bank to deny or refuse
> [L43] correction of a billing error solely because internal timelines have elapsed, where the denial
> [L44] itself resulted from defective notice, unreasonable investigation, or reliance on unenforceable
> [L45] merchant assertions.
> [L46] Regulation Z (12 CFR §1026.13) does not create a safe harbor for procedural closure. It
> [L47] requires a reasonable investigation and a determination based on whether the goods or
> [L48] services were actually delivered. A dispute denied through defective notice or ignored
> [L49] evidence does not become lawful merely by passage of time.

> [L33] without addressing non-delivery. The bank later closed the dispute for

> [L35] knowing I was traveling and despite my repeated requests for digital
> [L36] delivery. No alternative notice was provided. The bank also relied on
> [L37] unsigned, post-payment merchant terms that I never agreed to. There is no
> [L38] evidence of assent. Such terms cannot cure non-delivery. These actions

> # 【5†FIB DBFI - Gmail - Consumer Complaint - First Interstate Bank.pdf†file_00000000d33c71fd8310f16996ce24c4】
> Content source: Source.file
> Title: FIB DBFI - Gmail - Consumer Complaint - First Interstate Bank.pdf
> Citation Marker: fileciteturn2file5
> Mclick Target: "2:5"
> Content Snippet:
> [L1] Reg Z §1026.13(f) requires the issuer to provide documentation upon the consumer’s request. I have requested
> [L2] merchant documentation repeatedly - verbally on recorded calls, in writing, and through the CFPB portal. FIB has
> [L3] refused every time. This is not a gray area. This is a named, specific statutory obligation FIB has willfully not fulfilled.
> [L4] 2. Timely investigation - violated by procedural manipulation.
> [L5] FIB issued a denial based on a sub-7-day alleged response window while I was traveling - travel FIB had on record
> [L6] through multiple card-unlock calls, recordings, and their own fraud monitoring system. The merchant received 60- 90+
> [L7] days. I received fewer than 7, with no verified proof the letter was ever sent or received. That is not a timely and
> [L8] reasonable process - it is a procedural trap.Gmail - Consumer Complaint - First Interstate Bank 3/7/26, 7:40 PM
> [L9] https://mail.google.com/mail/u/0/?ik=6ad66b5443&view=pt&search=…l=msg-a:r-1360893119810118358&simpl=msg-a:r2979508502533843971 Page 5 of 18
> [L10] 3. Written explanation - incomplete and evasive.
> [L11] FIB’s “need more information” letter did not specify what information was needed, despite me having already
> [L12] submitted substantial documented evidence. A written explanation that requests unspecified information while ignoring
> [L13] existing submitted evidence does not satisfy the spirit or letter of Reg Z’s written explanation requirement.
> [L14] This dispute is not about FIB being required to rule in my favor. It is about FIB being required to actually conduct the
> [L15] investigation Reg Z mandates - using the evidence provided, supplying documentation as required, and affording the
> [L16] consumer a reasonable opportunity to respond. FIB has failed all three.
> [L17] The documentation recently produced by First Interstate Bank confirms that the bank relied upon merchant
> [L18] evidence during the investigation that was never provided to me despite repeated requests. Regulation Z
> [L19] §1026.13(f) requires issuers to provide documentary evidence relied upon in making the determination when
> [L20] requested by the consumer. Without access to those materials, I was deprived of any meaningful opportunity to
> [L21] review or rebut the evidence used to deny my dispute.
> [L22] Please escalate accordingly.
> [L23] I respectfully request that the Division reopen this complaint for further review in light of the above. I am happy to
> [L24] provide any additional documentation the Division requires.
> [L25] … 
> [L26] Newly Produced Documentation After Regulatory Escalation
> [L27] It is also important to note that First Interstate Bank has now produced documentation that was repeatedly requested
> [L28] by me for months but was never provided during the dispute investigation process.
> [L29] This documentation was only produced after escalation to the Montana Division of Banking and Financial Institutions.
> [L30] Throughout the dispute process I repeatedly requested:
> [L31] • Merchant representment documentation
> [L32] • Evidence relied upon to deny the dispute
> [L33] • Documentation supporting the merchant’s claims
> [L34] These requests were made:
> [L35] • Verbally on recorded calls
> [L36] • In writing through correspondence
> [L37] • Through CFPB complaint filings
> [L38] • Through certified mail correspondence
> [L39] Despite these repeated requests, FIB refused to provide the requested documentation during the investigation period.
> [L40] Only after regulatory escalation was documentation finally produced.Gmail - Consumer Complaint - First Interstate Bank 3/7/26, 7:40 PM
> [L41] https://mail.google.com/mail/u/0/?ik=6ad66b5443&view=pt&search=…l=msg-a:r-1360893119810118358&simpl=msg-a:r2979508502533843971 Page 6 of 18
> [L42] This raises an additional compliance concern.
> [L43] If documentation existed that the bank relied upon in making its determination, Regulation Z §1026.13(f) requires that
> [L44] documentation be made available to the consumer upon request.

> [L1] the responsible 
> [L2] department, and date/time stamps (intake, provisional credit, requests, chargeback 
> [L3] initiation, representment receipt, deadlines set, closure decision) 
> [L4] ● ☐ Copies of all notices allegedly sent (including envelopes, address used, mail method, 
> [L5] and proof of mailing/delivery or returned mail) 
> [L6] V. Remedy & Requested Cure 
> [L7] V.A Remedy (Narrow, Reasonable, Proportional) 
> [L8] ● ☐ Reverse the remaining $10,500 (billing correction / permanent credit) 
> [L9] ● ☐ Remove all interest and fees 
> [L10] ● ☐ Restore account to pre-dispute status 
> [L11] ● ☐ Provide written confirmation 
> [L12] ● ☐ Close CFPB complaints as resolved 
> [L13] V.B Compliance Timeline (10 business days) 
> [L14] Administrative correction within a reasonable compliance timeframe of 10 business days will 
> [L15] fully resolve this matter without the need for further regulatory or judicial escalation. 
> [L16] Response required within 10 business days: written confirmation of 
> [L17] (1) permanent credit/refund of the remaining $10,500, 
> [L18] (2) interest/fees fully reversed to $0 related to the dispute, 
> [L19] (3) account restored to pre-dispute status, and 
> [L20] (4) date-stamped production of the complete case log + full representment packet. 
> [L21] V.C Required Issuer Escalation Actions 
> [L22] To cure the violations identified above and mitigate further exposure, First Interstate Bank must: 
> [L23] ☐ Initiate issuer-side escalation through applicable card network dispute channels 
> [L24] ☐ Submit pre-arbitration or equivalent escalation where merchant representment relies on 
> [L25] unenforceable or undisclosed terms 
> [L26] ☐ Permanently restore provisional credit pending final resolution 
> [L27] ☐ Maintain the account in status quo: no interest accrual, fees, negative reporting, or adverse 
> [L28] action while the dispute is under investigation and escalation 
> [L29] ☐ Cease interest accrual, fees, or adverse account treatment immediately 
> [L30] 13460363.1.10-12Failure to pursue issuer-side escalation where merchant claims are legally defective constitutes 
> [L31] an additional violation of the reasonable investigation requirement under FCBA §1666 and 
> [L32] Regulation Z §1026.13. 
> [L33] V.D. Jurisdiction, Agency, and Enforcement Preservation 
> [L34] This correspondence preserves all rights, remedies, and jurisdictions available under federal 
> [L35] and state law. Nothing herein shall be construed as a waiver of the right to pursue or continue 
> [L36] parallel review or enforcement before the Consumer Financial Protection Bureau (CFPB), the 
> [L37] Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation (FDIC), applicable State Attorneys General(s), United 
> [L38] States Attorney General, FTC, governing enforcement agencies, state banking regulators 
> [L39] including the Department of Banking and Financial Institutions (DBFI), or to seek relief in state 
> [L40] or federal court. 
> [L41] This notice is provided to afford First Interstate Bank a final administrative opportunity to correct 
> [L42] the billing error and mitigate escalating regulatory, supervisory, and judicial exposure. 
> [L43] Administrative correction does not foreclose, condition, or limit any pending or future regulatory 
> [L44] review or court action. 
> [L45] VI. Preservation / Litigation Hold 
> [L46] First Interstate Bank is hereby directed to preserve all records related to 
> [L47] this dispute, including internal case notes, escalation logs, call recordings, 
> [L48] merchant communications, mailing records, and decision rationales. 
> [L49] Failure to preserve such records after notice will be treated as spoliation 
> [L50] and presented to regulators or the court if necessary. 
> [L51] VII. Final Note 
> [L52] FINAL NOTE (IMPORTANT) 
> [L53] Nothing above relies on emotion, opinion, or speculation. 
> [L54] Every claim is either: 
> [L55] ● A verifiable fact 
> [L56] ● A documented assertion 
> [L57] ● Or a demand for proof the merchant/bank must produce This structure maximizes legal clarity, minimizes exposure for the bank, and supports 
> [L58] administrative correction without litigation.

> [L7] FTC Complaint - Merchant Fraud / Services Not Rendered I am submitting this complaint against Steam and Stream and its
> [L8] principal Khaleel Gonzalez for fraud, fraudulent inducement, and deceptive business practices. In or around 06/2025, I paid
> [L9] $18,000 for a Done-For-You Airbnb acquisition and deployment service for 2 DFY properties generating $3500/mo profit each after
> [L10] expenses; marketed and sold by Steam and Stream & the owner Khaleel Gonzalez. The merchant expressly promised to acquire a
> [L11] landlord-approved Airbnb property, furnish and deploy it, launch a live Airbnb listing, and generate revenue, with advertised claims
> [L12] of $10,000+ per month performance and $3,500+ monthly profit per property. Critically, the merchant represented in writing and
> [L13] sales materials that “you pay $0 if we do not find you a property.” This representation was material to my decision to purchase.
> [L14] Despite accepting full payment: No property was ever acquired No lease was secured No furnishing or deployment occurred No
> [L15] Airbnb listing ever went live No revenue was generated No promised Done-For-You service was delivered After repeatedly missing
> [L16] timelines, the merchant admitted non-delivery and issued a partial refund of $7,500, leaving $10,500 wrongfully retained despite
> [L17] complete failure to perform. This partial refund constitutes an acknowledgment of non-delivery. Instead of refunding the remaining
> [L18] balance, the merchant later attempted to justify keeping my money by asserting unsigned, post-payment “terms” and retroactive
> [L19] billing concepts that were never disclosed, never agreed to, and never signed. These terms were fabricated after payment and are
> [L20] unenforceable. The merchant also attempted to characterize delivery as temporary access to a low-cost third-party analytics
> [L21] software. This does not constitute delivery of a high-value Done-For-You business deployment and is a token gesture designed to
> [L22] create the appearance of performance. This conduct constitutes: Fraudulent inducement Material misrepresentation Failure of
> [L23] consideration Unjust enrichment Deceptive acts and practices Relevant legal violations include, but are not limited to: FTC Act §5
> [L24] (Unfair or Deceptive Acts or Practices) Fraud and misrepresentation under common law False advertising and deceptive marketing
> [L25] Retention of funds after admitted non-delivery The merchant’s written promise that no payment is owed if a property is not secured,
> [L26] combined with total non-performance and partial refund admission, makes retention of the remaining funds indefensible. Steam
> [L27] and Stream and Khaleel Gonzalez accepted my money, failed to deliver the promised service, partially refunded only after admitting
> [L28] failure, and then attempted to retain the remaining funds through deception. This is not a business dispute or quality issue - it is
> [L29] outright non-delivery and fraud. I respectfully request that the FTC investigate this merchant for deceptive practices, require
> [L30] restitution of the remaining $10,500+ accrued interest, and take appropriate enforcement action to prevent further consumer harm. I

> [L49] convert it into Yes/No admissions that corner the bank legally.This complaint concerns First Interstate Bank’s violations of Regulation Z (12 CFR §1026.13)
> [L50] and Dodd-Frank UDAAP standards in its handling of my credit card billing dispute.
> [L51] This submission is not a duplicate of CFPB Case 260131-28391819.
> [L52] That prior complaint addressed the underlying merchant transaction.
> [L53] This complaint addresses First Interstate Bank’s unlawful dispute handling, procedural
> [L54] misconduct, and continued noncompliance after notice.
> [L55] First Interstate Bank denied a services-not-rendered dispute without identifying any evidence
> [L56] that the contracted service was delivered. No property was delivered, no Airbnb listing
> [L57] launched, no revenue generated, and the merchant issued a partial refund acknowledging
> [L58] non-delivery. Despite this undisputed record, the bank denied the dispute and continues to
> [L59] charge interest.
> [L60] The bank relied on unsigned, post-payment merchant terms, refused to provide the
> [L61] merchant’s rebuttal documentation, and refused to disclose the dates the rebuttal was received,
> [L62] processed, or reviewed. Without these records, neither the consumer nor the CFPB can verify
> [L63] compliance with Regulation Z’s reasonable investigation requirements.
> [L64] The bank allowed the merchant approximately 60-90+ days to respond (Aug 13, 2025 to Nov
> [L65] 13, 2025), while giving me fewer than 10 days to respond, exclusively by physical mail, during
> [L66] a period when the bank knew I was traveling and after I requested digital delivery multiple times.

> [L2] assertions is not independently supportable without documentary proof. 2. Merchant Representment Packet Withheld
> [L3] After Repeated Requests The bank states the merchant responded on October 30, 2025 and that it relied upon those
> [L4] materials in denying the dispute. I have repeatedly requested: - The complete merchant representment packet - All
> [L5] attachments referenced - The full internal dispute case log with date/time stamps These materials have not been
> [L6] produced. Regulation Z requires that, upon request, the issuer provide copies of documents relied upon in making its
> [L7] determination. Continued refusal or failure to provide the documentation relied upon prevents meaningful review of the
> [L8] investigation. If the issuer cannot produce the documentation it relied upon, the denial lacks a verifiable evidentiary
> [L9] basis. 3. November 14, 2025 Letter - No Proof of Mailing or Delivery The bank asserts a “Need More Information”
> [L10] letter was mailed November 14, 2025 and that the dispute was closed due to non-response. I have repeatedly requested
> [L11] documented proof of mailing and delivery. None has been provided. The bank has not produced: - Proof of mailing
> [L12] method - Tracking number - Certified mail receipt - Proof of delivery or returned mail - Copy of envelope and address
> [L13] used Closing a dispute for non-response without proof that notice was properly mailed and delivered deprives the
> [L14] consumer of a meaningful opportunity to respond. If proof of mailing and delivery cannot be produced, the procedural
> [L15] basis for closing the dispute is not independently supportable. 4. Statement Regarding Travel Notices - Verification
> [L16] Required The bank asserts it has “no record of a travel notice.” I called multiple times on recorded lines to clear fraud
> [L17] flags while traveling and notified the bank of my travel states on multiple occasions. Because the issuer has made a
> [L18] factual representation to the CFPB regarding account records, preservation and review of: - All call recordings - Fraud
> [L19] department notes - Internal account annotations - Travel-notice logs is necessary to verify the accuracy of statements
> [L20] made in this response. If internal recordings or logs reflect travel notifications, the statement made to the CFPB would
> [L21] be materially inaccurate. 5. Investigation Adequacy Under 12 CFR §1026.13 The denial appears based on merchantComplaint Detail 3/7/26, 7:44 PM

> [L1] Bottom Line
> [L2] First Interstate Bank’s response:
> [L3] ● avoids the delivery question
> [L4] ● enforces unsigned terms
> [L5] ● withholds required dates
> [L6] ● relies on non-receipt mail
> [L7] ● substitutes narrative for proof
> [L8] This is exactly the conduct Regulation Z and UDAAP exist to prevent.
> [L9] compress this into a 2–3 sentence CFPB “killer paragraph”, or
> [L10] convert it into Yes/No admissions that corner the bank legally.This complaint concerns First Interstate Bank’s violations of Regulation Z (12 CFR §1026.13)
> [L11] and Dodd-Frank UDAAP standards in its handling of my credit card billing dispute.
> [L12] This submission is not a duplicate of CFPB Case 260131-28391819.
> [L13] That prior complaint addressed the underlying merchant transaction.
> [L14] This complaint addresses First Interstate Bank’s unlawful dispute handling, procedural
> [L15] misconduct, and continued noncompliance after notice.
> [L16] First Interstate Bank denied a services-not-rendered dispute without identifying any evidence
> [L17] that the contracted service was delivered. No property was delivered, no Airbnb listing
> [L18] launched, no revenue generated, and the merchant issued a partial refund acknowledging
> [L19] non-delivery. Despite this undisputed record, the bank denied the dispute and continues to
> [L20] charge interest.
> [L21] The bank relied on unsigned, post-payment merchant terms, refused to provide the
> [L22] merchant’s rebuttal documentation, and refused to disclose the dates the rebuttal was received,
> [L23] processed, or reviewed. Without these records, neither the consumer nor the CFPB can verify
> [L24] compliance with Regulation Z’s reasonable investigation requirements.
> [L25] The bank allowed the merchant approximately 60-90+ days to respond (Aug 13, 2025 to Nov
> [L26] 13, 2025), while giving me fewer than 10 days to respond, exclusively by physical mail, during
> [L27] a period when the bank knew I was traveling and after I requested digital delivery multiple times.
> [L28] The bank alleges a letter dated Nov 14, 2025 and closed the dispute for non-response by Nov
> [L29] 24, 2025. I have never received the alleged letters, yet the dispute was closed anyway.
> [L30] Timeline compliance does not excuse this conduct. Regulation Z requires a reasonable
> [L31] investigation and an evidence-based determination. There is no safe harbor allowing a
> [L32] bank to deny a billing error dispute based on procedure when the underlying facts establish
> [L33] non-delivery. A procedurally timely denial that ratifies merchant non-performance is not
> [L34] compliance - it is facilitation.
> [L35] The core defect is outcome logic. First Interstate Bank denied a services-not-rendered dispute
> [L36] without any evidence of service delivery. No timeline argument can cure that defect.
> [L37] I am requesting immediate supervisory review and administrative correction. These
> [L38] Regulation Z and UDAAP violations persist after notice and appeal, warranting CFPB
> [L39] oversight.No Safe Harbor for Timeline Excuses (Regulation Z & UDAAP)
> [L40] First Interstate Bank continues to assert that it “cannot assist” because the matter is allegedly
> [L41] outside internal dispute or chargeback timeframes. This position is legally incorrect.
> [L42] There is no statute, regulation, or card-network rule that permits a bank to deny or refuse
> [L43] correction of a billing error solely because internal timelines have elapsed, where the denial
> [L44] itself resulted from defective notice, unreasonable investigation, or reliance on unenforceable
> [L45] merchant assertions.
> [L46] Regulation Z (12 CFR §1026.13) does not create a safe harbor for procedural closure. It
> [L47] requires a reasonable investigation and a determination based on whether the goods or
> [L48] services were actually delivered. A dispute denied through defective notice or ignored
> [L49] evidence does not become lawful merely by passage of time.

> [L22] We received your complaint. Thank you.
> [L23] We will review your complaint. Depending on what we find, we will typically:
> [L24] Send your complaint to the company for a response; or
> [L25] Send your complaint to another state or federal agency, or help you get in touch with your state or local consumer protection
> [L26] office; or
> [L27] Let you know if we need more information to continue our work.
> [L28] YOUR COMPLAINT
> [L29] Regulation Z & UDAAP Noncompliance - Failure to Investigate and Ongoing Violations This complaint concerns
> [L30] First Interstate Bank’s failure to conduct a reasonable investigation, and its willful misconduct, willful negligence,
> [L31] reckless disregard, and willful noncompliance with Regulation Z (12 CFR §1026.13) and Dodd-Frank UDAAP
> [L32] standards in handling my credit card billing dispute. This submission is not a duplicate of CFPB Case 260131-
> [L33] 28391819. That complaint addressed the underlying transaction. This complaint addresses the bank’s procedural and
> [L34] substantive violations during and after the dispute process. First Interstate Bank denied a services-not-rendered dispute
> [L35] without identifying any evidence that the contracted service was delivered. No property was delivered, no listing
> [L36] launched, no revenue generated, and the merchant issued a partial refund acknowledging non-delivery. Despite this
> [L37] undisputed record, the bank denied the dispute and continues to charge interest. The bank relied on unsigned, post￾payment merchant terms, refused to provide the merchant’s rebuttal documentation, and refused to disclose the dates
> [L38] the rebuttal was received, processed, or reviewed, preventing verification of compliance with Regulation Z. The bank
> [L39] gave the merchant approximately 60-90+ days to respond (Aug 13, 2025 to Nov 13, 2025), while giving me fewer
> [L40] than 10 days, by physical mail only, despite knowing I was traveling and after I requested digital delivery. I have
> [L41] never received the alleged November 14, 2025 letter, yet the dispute was closed anyway. The bank now claims it
> [L42] “cannot assist” because internal timelines have passed. This is legally false. Regulation Z provides no safe harbor for
> [L43] denying a billing error based on procedure when the investigation is unreasonable or when the underlying facts
> [L44] establish non-delivery. A procedurally timely denial that ratifies merchant non-performance is not compliance - it is
> [L45] facilitation. Temporary access to: AirDNA, marketplaces, or consultation does not constitute delivery of the promised
> [L46] DFY merchant services with an abundance of proof provided on the subject in detail, directly from the merchant
> [L47] themselves. Under Mastercard Reason Code 4853, the test is binary: Was the promised service delivered? Here, the
> [L48] answer is no. The promised service with provided proof explicitly states a DFY (Done-For-You) Airbnb acquired
> [L49] property; designed, furnished, & listed that profits $3,500-$5,000+/mo after operational expenses. Token or
> [L50] preparatory activity cannot cure total non-delivery. No timeline argument can cure that defect.

> [L2] $10,500 related to a Done-For-You (DFY) Airbnb service that was never delivered. I
> [L3] paid $18,000 for a fixed-fee service promising property acquisition, furnishing, Airbnb
> [L4] listing launch, and revenue generation. After 11+ weeks, no property, no listing, and no
> [L5] revenue existed. The merchant acknowledged non-delivery by issuing a $7,500 partial
> [L6] refund, leaving $10,500 outstanding. I submitted complete dispute documentation on
> [L7] September 5, 2025. Despite this, the bank: asserted “missing documentation” after
> [L8] receipt, relied on the merchant’s rebuttal instead of verifying delivery, and denied the
> [L9] dispute and appeal without addressing non-delivery. The bank later closed the dispute for
> [L10] “non-response” based on a physical letter I never received, despite knowing I was
> [L11] traveling and despite my repeated requests for digital delivery. No alternative notice was
> [L12] provided. The bank also relied on unsigned, post-payment merchant terms that I never
> [L13] agreed to. There is no evidence of assent. Such terms cannot cure non-delivery. These
> [L14] actions raise concerns under Regulation Z (12 CFR §1026.13) for failure to conduct a
> [L15] reasonable investigation and under Dodd-Frank UDAAP for unfair procedural handling
> [L16] causing consumer harm. I am requesting CFPB review of the bank’s compliance and
> [L17] assistance obtaining: refund of the remaining $10,500, and reversal of associated interest
> [L18] and fees. Further details, documents, and information attached below. Along with a
> [L19] simple Yes / No questionnaire to be filled be First Interstate Bank.
> [L20] What would be a fair resolution to this issue?
> [L21] A fair resolution is correction of a documented services-not-rendered billing error and
> [L22] restoration of my account to the position it would have been in had the error not
> [L23] occurred. Specifically, I am requesting: Immediate refund of the remaining $10,500 for
> [L24] the undelivered service Reversal of all interest and fees associated with the disputed
> [L25] amount Written confirmation that the dispute is resolved on the merits (non-delivery), not
> [L26] consumer fault Confirmation that no adverse or retaliatory action will be taken against
> [L27] my account for exercising dispute rights This resolution reflects the minimum corrective
> [L28] action required under Regulation Z (12 CFR §1026.13) and avoids unnecessary
> [L29] escalation. I reserve all rights related to consequential damages arising from this matter.

> [L31] CONSUMERFINANCE.GOV SO THAT OTHERS CAN LEARN FROM MY

## 🤖 ChatGPT

Yes. Current record is enough to build a strong **CFPB-ready complaint against FIB**.

## What the record already supports

Your file set shows:

- The underlying dispute was for a **$10,500 remaining balance** on an $18,000 fixed-fee DFY Airbnb program promising property acquisition, setup, listing launch, and revenue generation; your evidence says **no property, no live listing, and no revenue** occurred, and a **$7,500 partial refund** was issued. fileciteturn2file14L1-L18
- FIB’s position was that the merchant provided **129.5 service hours, 93 underwritten properties, and software access**, and that you failed to respond to a **November 14, 2025** letter. fileciteturn2file1L1-L19
- Your record also shows repeated complaints that FIB **withheld the merchant representment packet / case records for months**, and that proof of mailing or delivery for the November 14 letter was not produced during the dispute process. fileciteturn2file5L17-L30 fileciteturn2file11L3-L14
- The merchant materials you compiled describe the sale as a **fixed-fee DFY service**, and your packet frames the core issue as **services not rendered**, not a mere quality dispute. fileciteturn2file3L13-L30

Your screenshots also show the merchant’s **privacy-policy** and **terms** links returning **404 Page not found**, which is useful as supporting context on unstable / unavailable web terms.

## Best CFPB angle

Keep this complaint **narrow**:

**FIB failed to conduct and document a reasonable billing-error investigation, relied on disputed/unsigned merchant assertions, used alleged physical-mail notice to close the dispute, and failed to timely provide the records it claimed to rely on.**

That is stronger than making the CFPB complaint mainly about merchant fraud.

## What happened
Paste this:

> This complaint concerns First Interstate Bank’s handling of my credit-card billing-error dispute for a $10,500 remaining charge tied to a fixed-fee “done-for-you” Airbnb service that I contend was not delivered as agreed. The service was sold as a program involving property acquisition, furnishing/setup, listing launch, and revenue generation. I submitted documentation showing that no property was acquired, no Airbnb listing went live, no revenue was generated, and the merchant issued a $7,500 partial refund while retaining $10,500. fileciteturn2file14L1-L18
>
> First Interstate Bank denied the dispute after relying on merchant assertions that service hours, property analyses, and software access constituted performance, and also relied on an alleged November 14, 2025 “Need More Information” letter that I did not receive. FIB’s and DBFI-related records state that the bank treated my non-response to that letter as part of the basis for closing the dispute, while also asserting the merchant had provided 129.5 service hours, 93 professionally underwritten properties, and software access. fileciteturn2file1L1-L19
>
> My concern is not simply disagreement with the outcome. My concern is whether FIB conducted a reasonable and properly documented investigation. During and after the dispute process, I repeatedly requested the merchant representment materials, supporting documentation, and proof of mailing/delivery of the November 14 letter. My records state those materials were not provided during the investigation period and were only produced, at least in part, after state-regulatory escalation. fileciteturn2file5L17-L30
>
> The investigation also relied on disputed merchant “terms” and theories of performance that I did not accept as the governing agreement for the original purchase. My records further reflect that proof of mailing or delivery of the November 14, 2025 notice was not produced during the dispute process, even though FIB later relied on that alleged notice to claim non-response. fileciteturn2file11L3-L14
>
> I am requesting CFPB review of whether First Interstate Bank complied with its billing-error investigation duties, whether it gave me a meaningful opportunity to respond before closing the dispute, and whether it properly maintained and disclosed the records it claimed to rely on. I seek correction of the disputed $10,500 charge, reversal of related interest and fees, and written confirmation of the documents and investigation records relied upon in the determination. fileciteturn2file0L24-L33

## Two important refinements

First, I would **not** lead the CFPB complaint with “accomplice,” “fraud partner,” or similar language. For CFPB, stronger is:
- **failed to conduct a reasonable investigation**
- **relied on disputed merchant assertions**
- **closed the dispute based on alleged mailed notice**
- **failed to timely provide records relied upon**

Second, I would keep the 404 screenshots as **supporting evidence**, not the headline. They help show instability or non-availability of claimed web terms, but your best case against FIB is still:
- non-delivery evidence
- disputed merchant terms
- alleged Nov. 14 notice
- delayed / missing production of relied-on records

## One thing that would materially improve precision

Yes — extracting the following to text would help sharpen this further:

- the exact text of the merchant representment packet
- the exact text of the November 14, 2025 letter
- any denial / appeal letters from FIB
- any call notes or emails showing your requests for digital delivery / records

If you paste those, I’ll turn this into a **final, polished CFPB submission** with zero fluff and the tightest possible wording.

> [!abstract]- Tool
> Make sure to include fileciteturn3file0 in your response to cite this file, or to surface it as a link.
> 
> Legal Context - Enforceability of Online Terms
> Courts consistently hold that online terms are not enforceable where a consumer is not provided reasonably conspicuous notice of the terms and does not provide a clear and unambiguous manifestation of assent.
> Core Legal Principle
> Courts begin with a basic rule of contract law: a contract requires mutual assent (agreement).
> If a user did not receive reasonable notice of the terms and did not clearly and unambiguously manifest assent to those terms, courts find that no contract was formed at all.
> Plain meaning:
> A company cannot enforce hidden terms or policies if the customer was never clearly shown those terms and never clearly agreed to them.
> If the user never agreed to the Terms of Use, none of the provisions within those terms are binding.
> These decisions reinforce the established rule that browsewrap-style terms presented without clear notice and affirmative acceptance do not create enforceable agreements.
> ________________
> 
> 
> Key Court Decisions
> Nguyen v. Barnes & Noble Inc.
> 763 F.3d 1171 (9th Cir. 2014)
> The Ninth Circuit affirmed denial of arbitration, holding that a browsewrap hyperlink alone - even when visible near checkout buttons - is insufficient to bind a user absent actual notice or an unambiguous manifestation of assent.
> The court ruled that a hyperlink to “Terms of Use” on a website is not enough to bind a user when the user is not required to click agreement or otherwise clearly indicate acceptance of the terms.
> Plain meaning:
> Simply placing terms somewhere online on a website does not automatically make them binding if the customer never clearly and knowingly agreed to them.
> ________________
> 
> 
> Specht v. Netscape Communications Corp.
> 150 F. Supp. 2d 585 (S.D.N.Y. 2001)
> The court held that downloading software without being required to affirmatively click “I agree” does not constitute assent to hidden or inconspicuous license terms, and therefore arbitration could not be compelled.
> The court specifically held that downloading software alone is not an unambiguous manifestation of assent, particularly where the license terms were not clearly presented to the user.
> Plain meaning:
> A company cannot claim you agreed to hidden terms simply because you used or downloaded something.
> ________________
> 
> 
> Berman v. Freedom Financial Network, LLC
> More recently, the Ninth Circuit reaffirmed that consumers cannot be bound by contractual or arbitration provisions embedded in website terms where the interface fails to clearly inform users that they are agreeing to those terms and fails to obtain an unambiguous manifestation of assent.
> The court emphasized that website terms are enforceable only when users receive clear notice of the terms and provide unambiguous agreement to them.
> Where terms are hidden, buried in hyperlinks, or never clearly accepted by the user, courts find that no binding agreement exists.
> ________________
> 
> 
> Required Elements for Enforceable Website Terms
> Courts consistently require both of the following:
> 1. Reasonably conspicuous notice of the terms, and
> 2. An unambiguous manifestation of assent by the user
> If either element is missing, courts generally refuse enforcement.
> If a website does not provide reasonably conspicuous notice of the terms and obtain the user’s clear and unambiguous agreement, courts generally find that no contract was formed and the terms are not enforceable.
> Hidden terms, buried hyperlinks, or terms that a user never clearly and affirmatively agreed to are therefore not binding.
> Courts routinely refuse to enforce website terms where the interface design fails to provide sufficiently conspicuous notice and fails to obtain clear and unambiguous user assent.
> ________________
> 
> 
> U.S. Supreme Court Principle
> These rulings reflect a foundational contract principle recognized by the U.S. Supreme Court: arbitration is a matter of consent and cannot be imposed absent a valid agreement.
> See AT&T Technologies, Inc. v. Communications Workers of America:
> “Arbitration is a matter of contract and a party cannot be required to submit to arbitration any dispute which he has not agreed so to submit.”
> Plain meaning:
> A company cannot force someone into contract terms they never clearly and knowingly agreed to.
> ________________
> 
> 
> What This Means for Online Terms
> If a court finds that a user never clearly and unambiguously agreed to the Terms of Use, the entire set of terms may be unenforceable, including, but not limited to:
> * deliverables
> * arbitration clauses
> * liability limitations
> * class action waivers
> * merchant “terms and conditions” or policy provisions
> Plain meaning:
> If the customer never clearly agreed to the terms, the company cannot rely on those terms to limit responsibility or enforce policies.
> ________________
> 
> 
> Bottom Line
> Courts repeatedly reject attempts by companies to enforce website terms or policies when:
> * the terms were hidden or difficult to notice, and
> * the consumer never provided clear and unambiguous agreement to those terms
> Simple takeaway:
> Hidden terms or “you agreed by using the website” policies often do not hold up in court without clear notice and unambiguous consent.
> ________________
> 
> 
> Judicial Standard Applied by Courts
> Courts evaluating online terms repeatedly emphasize that a user must be placed on clear notice of the terms and must provide an unambiguous manifestation of assent before those terms become binding. As the Second Circuit explained in Specht v. Netscape Communications Corp.:
> “A reasonably prudent Internet user would not have known or learned of the existence of the license terms … and therefore did not unambiguously manifest assent to those terms.”
> This principle is repeatedly applied in cases such as Nguyen v. Barnes & Noble Inc. and Berman v. Freedom Financial Network, LLC, where courts refused to enforce website terms because users were not given clear notice and did not clearly agree to them.
> Plain meaning:
> If a person online does not clearly understand that they are agreeing to specific terms, and are proven to have agreed to those specific terms, courts generally find that no binding agreement exists.
> If a person online is not unambiguously made aware of clearly specific terms, and clearly, indisputably consents to & agrees with those specific terms, courts find that no binding agreement exists.
> 
> 
> 
> 
> ________________
> 
> 
> 
> 
> CONCLUSION / SUMMARY
> Courts do not ask whether a merchant technically displayed terms somewhere on a website. Instead, they examine whether a reasonably prudent user was placed on reasonably conspicuous notice of specific terms and clearly and unambiguously manifested assent to those terms.
> If a user is not clearly made aware of specific terms from the user’s perspective and experience, and does not clearly and unambiguously agree to those terms, courts generally find that no binding agreement exists - regardless of whether the merchant claims the terms were technically displayed, delivered, or merely available on the website.
> Availability of terms alone does not create assent; clear notice and unambiguous agreement are required for a binding contract to exist.
