> [L34] continued refusal to produce the materials relied upon raises a material question regarding compliance with 12 CFR
> [L35] §1026.13.
> # 【2†FIB CFPB 3 Attachments Combined.pdf†file_00000000f41871fdb9e9c5a99e84bed8】
> Content source: Source.file
> Title: FIB CFPB 3 Attachments Combined.pdf
> Citation Marker: fileciteturn4file2
> Mclick Target: "4:2"
> Content Snippet:
> [L1] Sept 25, 2025
> [L2] Provisional credit issued.
> [L3] Oct 30, 2025
> [L4] Issuer states merchant provided rebuttal documentation.
> [L5] Nov 14, 2025
> [L6] Issuer claims a “Need More Information” letter was mailed.
> [L7] Nov 24, 2025
> [L8] Issuer claims dispute closed for non-response.
> [L9] However:
> [L10] ● I never received the alleged November 14 letter
> [L11] ● the issuer cannot provide proof of mailing or delivery
> [L12] ● the issuer refuses to provide the merchant documentation it relied upon
> [L13] Regulatory Deficiencies1. Refusal to Produce Documentation Relied Upon
> [L14] The issuer states that merchant documentation was used to deny the dispute.
> [L15] Despite repeated written requests, the issuer has not produced:
> [L16] ● the complete merchant representment packet
> [L17] ● attachments referenced in the denial
> [L18] ● the dispute case log with timestamps
> [L19] ● the internal review documentation supporting the decision
> [L20] Without production of these materials, the reasonableness of the investigation required under
> [L21] 12 CFR §1026.13 cannot be independently verified.
> [L22] An investigation that cannot be verified based on documentary evidence raises a material
> [L23] compliance concern.
> [L24] Consumer Financial Protection Act (CFPA) Compliance
> [L25] Concerns
> [L26] The conduct described above raises potential compliance concerns under the Consumer
> [L27] Financial Protection Act’s prohibition on unfair, deceptive, or abusive acts or practices
> [L28] (UDAAP).
> [L29] ● 12 USC 5531 (UDAAP)
> [L30] ● 12 USC 5565 (civil penalties)
> [L31] Consumer Financial Protection Act
> [L32] Under the statute:
> [L33] “The Bureau may take any action… to prevent a covered person from committing or
> [L34] engaging in an unfair, deceptive, or abusive act or practice.”
> [L35] Source
> [L36] https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/12/5531
> [L37] The prolonged refusal to provide investigation documentation relied upon in a dispute
> [L38] determination, despite repeated documented requests and subsequent regulatory intervention,raises concerns regarding whether the issuer’s dispute-handling practices could result in
> [L39] avoidable consumer harm or procedural unfairness.
> [L40] Potential Regulatory Enforcement Authority
> [L41] If supervisory review identifies violations of the Consumer Financial Protection Act, the Bureau
> [L42] has authority to pursue civil penalties under:
> [L43] Consumer Financial Protection Act
> [L44] Civil penalty tiers authorized by statute include:
> [L45] • Up to $5,000 per day for ordinary violations
> [L46] • Up to $25,000 per day for reckless violations
> [L47] • Up to $1,000,000 per day for knowing violations
> [L48] Source
> [L49] https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/12/5565
> [L50] These penalties apply when regulators determine that covered financial institutions have
> [L51] engaged in conduct that violates federal consumer financial law.
> [L52] Additional Procedural Deficiencies and
> [L53] Notice Issues
> [L54] The issuer’s failure to produce the documentation relied upon occurred despite repeated
> [L55] documented requests over an extended period of time.
> [L56] Specifically, the requested investigation materials were sought through multiple channels,
> [L57] including:
> [L58] • Certified mail correspondence
> [L59] • Fax transmissions
> [L60] • Recorded telephone calls with bank representativesDespite these repeated documented requests, the issuer refused or failed to produce the
> [L61] requested materials for approximately seven months following the initial dispute filed in
> [L62] August 2025.
> [L63] The requested documentation was only produced after intervention by the state regulator,
> [L64] the Division of Banking and Financial Institutions (DBFI).
> [L65] Prior to that regulatory involvement, the issuer ignored or declined multiple consumer requests
> [L66] for the materials it claims to have relied upon in denying the billing error.
> [L67] This extended refusal to produce investigation documentation further prevents independent
> [L68] verification that a reasonable investigation occurred as required under Regulation Z (12 CFR
> [L69] §1026.13).
> [L70] The fact that the documentation was produced only after regulatory intervention further
> [L71] raises questions regarding the issuer’s repeated patterns of dispute-handling procedures
> [L72] and recordkeeping practices.
> [L73] Notice and Opportunity to Respond
> [L74] The issuer also closed the dispute for “non-response” based on an alleged November 14, 2025
> [L75] letter, which the issuer claims was mailed.

> Mclick Target: "4:3"
> Content Snippet:
> [L1] I have repeatedly requested documented
> [L2] proof of mailing and delivery. None has been provided. The bank has not produced: - Proof of mailing method -
> [L3] Tracking number - Certified mail receipt - Proof of delivery or returned mail - Copy of envelope and address used
> [L4] Closing a dispute for non-response without proof that notice was properly mailed and delivered deprives the
> [L5] consumer of a meaningful opportunity to respond. If proof of mailing and delivery cannot be produced, the
> [L6] procedural basis for closing the dispute is not independently supportable. 4. Statement Regarding Travel Notices -
> [L7] Verification Required The bank asserts it has “no record of a travel notice.” I called multiple times on recorded lines
> [L8] to clear fraud flags while traveling and notified the bank of my travel states on multiple occasions. Because the
> [L9] issuer has made a factual representation to the CFPB regarding account records, preservation and review of: - All
> [L10] call recordings - Fraud department notes - Internal account annotations - Travel-notice logs is necessary to verify
> [L11] the accuracy of statements made in this response. If internal recordings or logs reflect travel notifications, the
> [L12] statement made to the CFPB would be materially inaccurate. 5. Investigation Adequacy Under 12 CFR §1026.13
> [L13] The denial appears based on merchant narrative rather than documented proof of agreed contractual terms or actual
> [L14] delivery of the promised service. Without production of: - The executed governing agreement - The documentation
> [L15] relied upon in denial - The complete representment file - The dispute case log with timestamps Proof of proper
> [L16] notice the reasonableness of the investigation cannot be independently evaluated. Once these deficiencies were
> [L17] raised and documentation was repeatedly requested, continued refusal to produce the materials relied upon raises a
> [L18] material question regarding compliance with 12 CFR §1026.13. Requested CFPB Action I respectfully request that
> [L19] the CFPB require the issuer to produce: - Signed governing agreement relied upon - Signed hourly billing
> [L20] authorization (if claimed) - Complete merchant representment packet with attachments - Complete dispute case log
> # 【4†IR 2068-3.2.26 - Alan Nguyen - Complaint Response - State of MT-20260302173627.pdf†file_00000000a3a071f8ad5af8f0e1b45809】
> Content source: Source.file
> Title: IR 2068-3.2.26 - Alan Nguyen - Complaint Response - State of MT-20260302173627.pdf
> Citation Marker: fileciteturn4file4
> Mclick Target: "4:4"
> Content Snippet:
> [L1] Based on the 
> [L2] response there were additional items we needed you to address. Another ‘Need More 
> [L3] Information Letter’ was mailed on November 14, 2025, to the same address as the previous 
> [L4] letters. You did not respond to the November 14th letter on or prior to the established 
> [L5] deadline.
> [L6] I have spoken with our Credit Card Fraud Operations Team who advised me that First 
> [L7] Interstate has no record of a travel notice on your account or any notes indicating that you 
> [L8] were travelling during the timeframe in which dispute communication was sent to you by the 
> [L9] Bank. Regardless, records indicate you communicated to our Fraud Team that everything we 
> [L10] needed according to the second ‘Need More Information Letter’ was provided in your 
> [L11] response to the first letter.
> [L12] You raised issue with the reason for the chargeback being that the purchase was non￾refundable. Although you claim non-delivery, the merchant presented evidence to First 
> [L13] Interstate Bank that they provided 129.5 service hours, provided you with 93 professionally 
> [L14] underwritten properties in your market of choice, and purchased AirDNA PPRO Membership 
> [L15] and AirBitrage.ai property marketplace access for you in accordance with your agreement.Lastly, you claimed that the merchant cannot on one hand provide a refund and on the other 
> [L16] state the service is non-refundable. The merchant’s notes and text messages between the 
> [L17] merchant and you state that the refund was a result of a downgrade in the services from the 
> [L18] Enterprise Package to the Pro Package (2 properties to 1 property). After the refund, you 
> [L19] continued to work with the company, participating in consulting sessions for 45 days.
> [L20] I apologize for any frustration you may have experienced, and I am sorry that First Interstate 
> [L21] is unable to further assist you with this matter. We encourage you to continue to work 
> [L22] directly with the merchant to resolve your issue.
> [L23] Sincerely,
> [L24] Joey Liskh
> [L25] Director of Compliance
> [L26] Enc: February 11th Response Letter, Dispute Letters, Merchant Representment DocumentsCompliance Department
> [L27] PO Box 30918
> [L28] Billings, MT 59116
> [L29] 406-237-2688
> [L30] firstinterstate.com
> [L31] February 11, 2026
> [L32] Alan Nguyen
> [L33] 11150 Rhode Island Ave S
> [L34] Bloomington, MN 55438
> [L35] Re: Consumer Financial Protection Bureau Case 260131-28391819
> [L36] Dear Mr. Nguyen,
> [L37] I recently received your complaint submitted to the Consumer Financial Protection Bureau, 
> [L38] and I have conducted research regarding your claims.
> [L39] Enclosed you will find a copy of all dispute related correspondence sent to you by First 
> [L40] Interstate Bank. Records indicate that all applicable policies and procedures were properly 
> [L41] followed. 
> [L42] Per our Credit Card Operations Department, the merchant provided representment 
> [L43] documents, within the allowable timeframe, indicating the transaction in question was non￾refundable. Additionally, you did not provide a response by the date requested in our 
> [L44] November 14th Merchant Rebuttal Letter. After reviewing the representment documents and 
> [L45] all other available supporting documentation, the investigation was concluded and a letter 
> [L46] was sent indicating our Dispute Department was unable to further pursue the issue.
> [L47] I am sorry that we are unable to further assist you with this matter. We encourage you to 
> [L48] continue to work directly with the merchant to resolve your issue. Should you have any 
> [L49] additional questions or concerns regarding your dispute or to obtain additional supporting 
> [L50] information/documentation, we ask that you please contact our Dispute Department directly
> [L51] at 1-800-257-9620.
> [L52] Sincerely,
> [L53] Kelli Asay
> [L54] Compliance Officer
> [L55] Enc: Acknowledgment Letter, Need More Info Letter, Provisional Credit Letter, Merchant 
> [L56] Rebuttal Letter, No Rule Letters (Nov. 2025 & Dec. 2025)
> # 【5†FIB CFPB 3 Attachments Combined.pdf†file_00000000f41871fdb9e9c5a99e84bed8】
> Content source: Source.file
> Title: FIB CFPB 3 Attachments Combined.pdf
> Citation Marker: fileciteturn4file5
> Mclick Target: "4:5"
> Content Snippet:
> [L1] Despite
> [L2] repeated direct requests, no executed agreement, no merchant representment packet, and no proof of mailing of the
> [L3] November 14 letter has been produced. These deficiencies prevent independent verification of investigation
> [L4] adequacy under 12 CFR §1026.13. The materials identified below have been requested multiple times directly from
> [L5] the bank. The bank has refused or failed to produce them. 1. Executed Governing Agreement Not Produced The
> [L6] denial relies on alleged merchant terms including hourly billing, non-refundability, and modified service
> [L7] characterization. Despite repeated written requests, the bank has not produced: - Any signed governing agreement -
> [L8] Any signed authorization for hourly billing - Any executed agreement establishing non-refundable terms - Any
> [L9] document evidencing assent to modified deliverables If the issuer cannot produce an executed agreement supporting
> [L10] the terms relied upon, the investigation cannot be independently verified as reasonable under 12 CFR §1026.13. An
> [L11] investigation that relies on unsigned or unexecuted merchant assertions is not independently supportable without
> [L12] documentary proof. 2. Merchant Representment Packet Withheld After Repeated Requests The bank states the
> [L13] merchant responded on October 30, 2025 and that it relied upon those materials in denying the dispute. I have
> [L14] repeatedly requested: - The complete merchant representment packet - All attachments referenced - The full internal
> [L15] dispute case log with date/time stamps These materials have not been produced. Regulation Z requires that, upon
> [L16] request, the issuer provide copies of documents relied upon in making its determination. Continued refusal or
> [L17] failure to provide the documentation relied upon prevents meaningful review of the investigation. If the issuer
> [L18] cannot produce the documentation it relied upon, the denial lacks a verifiable evidentiary basis. 3. November 14,
> [L19] 2025 Letter - No Proof of Mailing or Delivery The bank asserts a “Need More Information” letter was mailed
> [L20] November 14, 2025 and that the dispute was closed due to non-response. I have repeatedly requested documented
> [L21] proof of mailing and delivery. None has been provided. The bank has not produced: - Proof of mailing method -
> [L22] Tracking number - Certified mail receipt - Proof of delivery or returned mail - Copy of envelope and address used
> [L23] Closing a dispute for non-response without proof that notice was properly mailed and delivered deprives the
> [L24] consumer of a meaningful opportunity to respond. If proof of mailing and delivery cannot be produced, the
> [L25] procedural basis for closing the dispute is not independently supportable. 4. Statement Regarding Travel Notices -
> [L26] Verification Required The bank asserts it has “no record of a travel notice.” I called multiple times on recorded lines
> [L27] to clear fraud flags while traveling and notified the bank of my travel states on multiple occasions. Because the
> [L28] issuer has made a factual representation to the CFPB regarding account records, preservation and review of: - All
> [L29] call recordings - Fraud department notes - Internal account annotations - Travel-notice logs is necessary to verify
> [L30] the accuracy of statements made in this response. If internal recordings or logs reflect travel notifications, the
> [L31] statement made to the CFPB would be materially inaccurate. 5. Investigation Adequacy Under 12 CFR §1026.13
> [L32] The denial appears based on merchant narrative rather than documented proof of agreed contractual terms or actual
> [L33] delivery of the promised service. Without production of: - The executed governing agreement - The documentation
> [L34] relied upon in denial - The complete representment file - The dispute case log with timestamps Proof of proper
> [L35] notice the reasonableness of the investigation cannot be independently evaluated. Once these deficiencies were
> [L36] raised and documentation was repeatedly requested, continued refusal to produce the materials relied upon raises a
> [L37] material question regarding compliance with 12 CFR §1026.13.

> [L34] representation to the CFPB regarding account records that may require verification. Despite repeated direct requests, no
> [L35] executed agreement, no merchant representment packet, and no proof of mailing of the November 14 letter has been
> [L36] produced. These deficiencies prevent independent verification of investigation adequacy under 12 CFR §1026.13. The
> [L37] materials identified below have been requested multiple times directly from the bank. The bank has refused or failed to
> [L38] produce them. 1. Executed Governing Agreement Not Produced The denial relies on alleged merchant terms including
> [L39] hourly billing, non-refundability, and modified service characterization. Despite repeated written requests, the bank has
> [L40] not produced: - Any signed governing agreement - Any signed authorization for hourly billing - Any executed
> [L41] agreement establishing non-refundable terms - Any document evidencing assent to modified deliverables If the issuer
> [L42] cannot produce an executed agreement supporting the terms relied upon, the investigation cannot be independently
> [L43] verified as reasonable under 12 CFR §1026.13. An investigation that relies on unsigned or unexecuted merchant
> [L44] assertions is not independently supportable without documentary proof. 2. Merchant Representment Packet Withheld
> [L45] After Repeated Requests The bank states the merchant responded on October 30, 2025 and that it relied upon those
> [L46] materials in denying the dispute. I have repeatedly requested: - The complete merchant representment packet - All
> [L47] attachments referenced - The full internal dispute case log with date/time stamps These materials have not been
> [L48] produced. Regulation Z requires that, upon request, the issuer provide copies of documents relied upon in making its
> [L49] determination. Continued refusal or failure to provide the documentation relied upon prevents meaningful review of the
> [L50] investigation. If the issuer cannot produce the documentation it relied upon, the denial lacks a verifiable evidentiary
> [L51] basis. 3. November 14, 2025 Letter - No Proof of Mailing or Delivery The bank asserts a “Need More Information”
> [L52] letter was mailed November 14, 2025 and that the dispute was closed due to non-response. I have repeatedly requested
> [L53] documented proof of mailing and delivery. None has been provided. The bank has not produced: - Proof of mailing
> [L54] method - Tracking number - Certified mail receipt - Proof of delivery or returned mail - Copy of envelope and address
> [L55] used Closing a dispute for non-response without proof that notice was properly mailed and delivered deprives the
> [L56] consumer of a meaningful opportunity to respond. If proof of mailing and delivery cannot be produced, the procedural
> [L57] basis for closing the dispute is not independently supportable. 4.

> [L1] Even if First Interstate Bank contends technical timeline compliance, federal law still requires
> [L2] correction where a billing error exists due to total non-delivery of services.
> [L3] This dispute does not hinge on timing. It hinges on undisputed facts:
> [L4] ● No property was secured
> [L5] ● No Airbnb listing went live
> [L6] ● No revenue was generated
> [L7] ● The merchant issued a partial refund acknowledging non-delivery
> [L8] Treating “consultation” or preparatory activity as fulfillment of a Done-For-You service is
> [L9] inconsistent with Regulation Z (12 CFR §1026.13) and constitutes an unfair practice when
> [L10] used to deny a documented services-not-rendered dispute.
> [L11] Further, reliance on unsigned, post-payment merchant terms to uphold a denial is legally
> [L12] unenforceable and inconsistent with reasonable investigation standards.
> [L13] I am requesting immediate administrative correction of the remaining $10,500, removal of
> [L14] associated interest, and written confirmation of resolution.
> [L15] Absent correction, this matter presents ongoing Reg Z and Dodd-Frank UDAAP compliance
> [L16] exposure appropriate for supervisory escalation.(3) LITIGATION HOLD & NOTICE OF CLAIMS
> [L17] Subject: Litigation Hold & Preservation Notice - Case ID D-252-230-3892
> [L18] Please treat this as formal notice.
> [L19] Due to First Interstate Bank’s continued refusal to correct a documented billing error involving
> [L20] non-delivery of services, I am providing notice of pending and imminent civil litigation.
> [L21] Accordingly, First Interstate Bank is required to preserve all records, including but not limited
> [L22] to:
> [L23] ● dispute intake and review logs
> [L24] ● merchant rebuttal receipt and processing dates
> [L25] ● internal communications, notes, and decision rationale
> [L26] ● correspondence delivery records and notice tracking
> [L27] Failure to preserve records after notice may result in adverse inference and additional liability.
> [L28] This notice is provided to allow resolution prior to litigation, should the bank elect to correct the

> [L67] evidence.Plain Statement of the Issue 
> [L68] This matter is straightforward. 
> [L69] I paid a merchant for a Done-For-You (DFY) Airbnb acquisition and deployment service that was 
> [L70] clearly promised, clearly defined, and never delivered. 
> [L71] The merchant accepted payment, failed to deliver the promised services, missed all agreed

> # 【11†FIB CFPB 2.pdf†file_00000000c1f871f8b8456f7b55b8179b】
> Content source: Source.file
> Title: FIB CFPB 2.pdf
> Citation Marker: fileciteturn4file11
> Mclick Target: "4:11"
> Content Snippet:
> [L1] Despite this
> [L2] undisputed record, the bank denied the dispute and continues to charge interest. The bank relied on unsigned, post￾payment merchant terms, refused to provide the merchant’s rebuttal documentation, and refused to disclose the dates
> [L3] the rebuttal was received, processed, or reviewed, preventing verification of compliance with Regulation Z. The bank
> [L4] gave the merchant approximately 60-90+ days to respond (Aug 13, 2025 to Nov 13, 2025), while giving me fewer
> [L5] than 10 days, by physical mail only, despite knowing I was traveling and after I requested digital delivery. I have
> [L6] never received the alleged November 14, 2025 letter, yet the dispute was closed anyway. The bank now claims it
> [L7] “cannot assist” because internal timelines have passed. This is legally false. Regulation Z provides no safe harbor for
> [L8] denying a billing error based on procedure when the investigation is unreasonable or when the underlying facts
> [L9] establish non-delivery. A procedurally timely denial that ratifies merchant non-performance is not compliance - it is
> [L10] facilitation. Temporary access to: AirDNA, marketplaces, or consultation does not constitute delivery of the promised
> [L11] DFY merchant services with an abundance of proof provided on the subject in detail, directly from the merchant
> [L12] themselves. Under Mastercard Reason Code 4853, the test is binary: Was the promised service delivered? Here, the
> [L13] answer is no. The promised service with provided proof explicitly states a DFY (Done-For-You) Airbnb acquired
> [L14] property; designed, furnished, & listed that profits $3,500-$5,000+/mo after operational expenses. Token or
> [L15] preparatory activity cannot cure total non-delivery. No timeline argument can cure that defect. First Interstate Bank
> [L16] continues to demonstrate willful negligence, and routinely ignores action relating to directly addressed compliance
> [L17] with UDAAP, REG Z, proper investigation, etc. They refuse to answer compliance related questions, and continue to
> [L18] violate consumer chargeback rights, and are abusing their powers as a bank, due to a lack of due diligence, and
> [L19] repeated documented willful misconduct. I am requesting immediate supervisory review and administrative
> [L20] correction. These Regulation Z and UDAAP violations persist after notice and appeal, warranting CFPB oversight.
> [L21] This must be escalated to all relevant enforcement agencies.

> # 【12†FIB x Steam and Stream - Combined.pdf†file_00000000180c71f8b511b9e4035543dc】
> Content source: Source.file
> Title: FIB x Steam and Stream - Combined.pdf
> Citation Marker: fileciteturn4file12
> Mclick Target: "4:12"
> Content Snippet:
> [L1] 1) Services Not Rendered
> [L2] 1.1) Verified Promises vs. Verified Non-Delivery
> [L3] This dispute is a services-not-rendered matter supported by
> [L4] - documented misrepresentation
> [L5] - failure of consideration
> [L6] - total non-delivery of the merchant’s core promised deliverables
> [L7] The merchant marketed and sold a Done-For-You (DFY) Airbnb acquisition and deployment
> [L8] system operation that promised:
> [L9] - rapid property acquisition
> [L10] - full furnishing and setup
> [L11] - professional listing launch
> [L12] - and immediate profitability.
> [L13] These promises are not interpretation
> [L14] They are documented in the merchant’s own:
> [L15] - public advertising
> [L16] - onboarding materials
> [L17] - Invoice
> [L18] - written marketing
> [L19] - recorded sales statements
> [L20] 61.2) Merchant’s Promises - Verifiable Evidence
> [L21] The merchant represented that the consumer would receive:
> [L22] • acquisition of a landlord-approved Airbnb property
> [L23] • full interior design and furnishing
> [L24] • professional photography
> [L25] • automated listing launch and optimization
> [L26] • bookings and revenue generation
> [L27] • target profit of $3,500-$5,000+ per month per property beginning month 1
> [L28] • ability to launch a $10K+ per month Airbnb first try
> [L29] • rapid execution timeline (weeks, not months)
> [L30] These representations are verified by:
> [L31] • merchant website screenshots advertising $10K+/month Airbnb launches
> [L32] • merchant promotional materials stating $3.5K+ monthly average profit
> [L33] • merchant visual timeline graphic promising rapid 90-day deployment
> [L34] • merchant branded PDF describing DFY acquisition + setup
> [L35] • recorded sales call transcript confirming week-by-week execution plan & profits
> [L36] • written flyer stating: “You pay $0 if we do not find you a property.”
> [L37] • the issued invoice documenting a fixed-fee DFY acquisition program
> [L38] Each of these items is attached and labeled for independent verification.
> [L39] They originate from the merchant, not the consumer.
> [L40] This establishes:
> [L41] - clear inducement terms
> [L42] - performance expectations
> [L43] - reliance
> [L44] 1.3) Verified Outcome After Eleven Weeks
> [L45] After more than eleven weeks from payment:
> [L46] • no property was acquired
> [L47] • no lease was secured
> [L48] • no furnishing occurred
> [L49] • no design work was completed
> [L50] • no photography was scheduled
> [L51] • no listing went live
> [L52] • no bookings occurred
> [L53] 7• no revenue was generated
> [L54] • no DFY system was deployed
> [L55] • no promised deliverable was fulfilled
> [L56] This is not partial performance.
> [L57] This is total non-delivery of the core service purchased.
> [L58] 1.4) Merchant’s Post-Payment Defense
> [L59] The merchant later attempted to justify non-delivery by:
> [L60] - introducing unsigned, post-payment “terms”
> [L61] - asserting retroactive hourly billing
> [L62] - presenting rules never disclosed
> [L63] - presenting documents never agreed to
> [L64] - presenting documents never signed
> [L65] The merchant also attempts to characterize delivery as:
> [L66] temporary access to shared AIRDNA software costing approximately
> [L67] $34-$125/month
> [L68] This software access:
> [L69] • expired after 3-4 weeks
> [L70] • is a low-cost third-party tool
> [L71] • provides inaccurate data
> [L72] • has trivial commercial value
> [L73] • does not replace the contracted service
> [L74] This is not performance.
> [L75] This is an attempt to manufacture the appearance of activity to defeat a chargeback.
> [L76] 1.4.A) Preparatory Activity Cannot Satisfy the Essential
> [L77] Purpose of the Contract
> [L78] The merchant may argue that access to analytics software or preliminary research constituted
> [L79] partial performance.
> [L80] Even assuming analytics research is a preparatory component of the workflow, preparatory
> [L81] steps do not equal delivery of the contracted service
> [L82] .
> [L83] The transaction was not for consulting, research assistance, or software access.
> [L84] 8The transaction was for a Done-For-You Airbnb acquisition and deployment resulting in an
> [L85] operational, furnished, revenue-generating property.
> [L86] Under contract law, performance is measured by fulfillment of the essential purpose of the
> [L87] agreement - not by isolated preparatory activity.
> # 【13†FIB x Steam and Stream - Combined.pdf†file_00000000180c71f8b511b9e4035543dc】
> Content source: Source.file
> Title: FIB x Steam and Stream - Combined.pdf
> Citation Marker: fileciteturn4file13
> Mclick Target: "4:13"
> Content Snippet:
> [L1] This establishes:
> [L2] - clear inducement terms
> [L3] - performance expectations
> [L4] - reliance
> [L5] 1.3) Verified Outcome After Eleven Weeks
> [L6] After more than eleven weeks from payment:
> [L7] • no property was acquired
> [L8] • no lease was secured
> [L9] • no furnishing occurred
> [L10] • no design work was completed
> [L11] • no photography was scheduled
> [L12] • no listing went live
> [L13] • no bookings occurred
> [L14] 7• no revenue was generated
> [L15] • no DFY system was deployed
> [L16] • no promised deliverable was fulfilled
> [L17] This is not partial performance.
> [L18] This is total non-delivery of the core service purchased.
> [L19] 1.4) Merchant’s Post-Payment Defense
> [L20] The merchant later attempted to justify non-delivery by:
> [L21] - introducing unsigned, post-payment “terms”
> [L22] - asserting retroactive hourly billing
> [L23] - presenting rules never disclosed
> [L24] - presenting documents never agreed to
> [L25] - presenting documents never signed
> [L26] The merchant also attempts to characterize delivery as:
> [L27] temporary access to shared AIRDNA software costing approximately
> [L28] $34-$125/month
> [L29] This software access:
> [L30] • expired after 3-4 weeks
> [L31] • is a low-cost third-party tool
> [L32] • provides inaccurate data
> [L33] • has trivial commercial value
> [L34] • does not replace the contracted service
> [L35] This is not performance.
> [L36] This is an attempt to manufacture the appearance of activity to defeat a chargeback.
> [L37] 1.4.A) Preparatory Activity Cannot Satisfy the Essential
> [L38] Purpose of the Contract
> [L39] The merchant may argue that access to analytics software or preliminary research constituted
> [L40] partial performance.
> [L41] Even assuming analytics research is a preparatory component of the workflow, preparatory
> [L42] steps do not equal delivery of the contracted service
> [L43] .
> [L44] The transaction was not for consulting, research assistance, or software access.
> [L45] 8The transaction was for a Done-For-You Airbnb acquisition and deployment resulting in an
> [L46] operational, furnished, revenue-generating property.
> [L47] Under contract law, performance is measured by fulfillment of the essential purpose of the
> [L48] agreement - not by isolated preparatory activity.
> [L49] See:
> [L50] • Restatement (Second) of Contracts §235
> [L51] • UCC failure of consideration principles
> [L52] • Texas contract law on essential purpose and material breach
> [L53] The essential purpose required:
> [L54] • acquisition of a landlord-approved property
> [L55] • furnishing and interior deployment
> [L56] • listing activation
> [L57] • operational launch
> [L58] • revenue generation capability
> [L59] None of these occurred.
> [L60] Where the essential purpose of a contract fails, preparatory work cannot convert
> [L61] non-performance into performance.
> [L62] Courts consistently reject the argument that incomplete preparation satisfies a contract whose
> [L63] central objective was never achieved.
> [L64] At most, analytics access represents abandoned preliminary activity that never matured into
> [L65] delivery.
> [L66] This is legally classified as:
> [L67] • failure of consideration
> [L68] • material breach
> [L69] • services not rendered
> [L70] • unjust enrichment if payment is retained
> [L71] A party cannot retain full payment by pointing to incomplete preparation when the contracted
> [L72] result was never delivered.
> [L73] That principle applies equally in contract law and card network dispute frameworks.
> [L74] 1.4.B) Token Activity Cannot Cure Total Non-Delivery
> [L75] 9Providing temporary access to low-cost third-party software does not constitute delivery of a
> [L76] high-value DFY deployment system.
> [L77] This is not partial fulfillment.
> [L78] This is an attempt to manufacture the appearance of activity to defeat chargeback eligibility.
> [L79] Mastercard dispute standards evaluate:
> [L80] - delivery of the contracted service
> [L81] - not peripheral activity
> [L82] - not unrelated tools
> [L83] - not token gestures
> [L84] Under Mastercard Reason Code 4853 (Goods or Services Not Provided), the test is binary:
> [L85] Was the promised service delivered as agreed?
> [L86] Here:
> [L87] - no property was acquired
> [L88] - no listing was launched
> [L89] - no system was operational
> [L90] - no revenue capability existed
> [L91] Therefore:
> [L92] the contracted service was not delivered.
> [L93] Token activity cannot substitute for contractual performance.
> [L94] Allowing merchants to defeat disputes by pointing to trivial or unrelated activity would nullify
> [L95] consumer protection frameworks entirely.
> [L96] Card network rules explicitly prevent this outcome.

> [L18] Regulation Z and improperly denied your services-not-rendered billing-error claim despite your position that no Airbnb
> [L19] property was acquired, no listing went live, no revenue was generated, and the merchant’s partial refund
> [L20] acknowledged non-delivery. Be advised that, according to the bank’s response and supporting timeline, the bank

> [L23] applicable time frames, documenting that it reviewed both your materials and the merchant’s evidence (including
> [L24] claimed service hours, property analyses, and access to software tools) before concluding that a billing error did not

> [L26] does not require the issuer to reach a particular substantive outcome or to resolve contractual disputes over the scope
> [L27] or quality of services in the consumer’s favor.

> [L37] timely manner or to the proper address.

also ensure that it is not a duplicate of 1 2 3 CFPB FIB complaints. we are aiming more towards what you mentioned in your initial response

Below are the **actual legal frameworks that govern banks when handling chargebacks and billing-error disputes.**
These are the statutes and regulations that **force banks to investigate properly and not simply accept a merchant’s claim.**

I will cite the controlling laws and the **exact obligations imposed on banks.**

---

# 1. The Primary Law: Fair Credit Billing Act (FCBA)

**Statute:**
15 U.S.C. §1666

The FCBA governs credit-card billing disputes and chargebacks.

It requires card issuers to conduct a **reasonable investigation** when a consumer disputes a charge.

### Key rule

A bank **cannot simply rely on the merchant’s statement**.

**Statutory language**

15 U.S.C. §1666(a):

> “If a creditor… receives a written notice from the obligor… the creditor shall investigate the matter and correct the billing error…”

Source
[https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/15/1666](https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/15/1666)

---

# 2. Regulation Z (FCBA Implementation Rule)

**Regulation:**
12 CFR §1026.13

This is the **federal rule banks must follow during disputes.**

### Investigation requirement

12 CFR §1026.13(f)

> “The creditor must conduct a reasonable investigation of the consumer’s claim.”

Source
[https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1026/13/](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1026/13/)

A bank violates federal law if it:

• ignores evidence from the consumer
• blindly accepts merchant claims
• fails to investigate conflicting evidence

---

# 3. CFPB Official Interpretation (Binding Guidance)

The Consumer Financial Protection Bureau clarified that **issuers cannot simply accept the merchant’s explanation**.

Official Interpretation §1026.13(f):

> “The creditor may not deny a claim solely because the merchant asserts that the charge is valid.”

Source
[https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1026/interp-13/](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1026/interp-13/)

This is critical.

It means:

A bank **must evaluate evidence independently**.

---

# 4. Error Type Covered by Law

Regulation Z defines billing errors including:

12 CFR §1026.13(a)(3)

> “A reflection on a statement of goods or services not accepted by the consumer or not delivered as agreed.”

Source
[https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1026/13/#a-3](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1026/13/#a-3)

This includes:

• non-delivery
• misrepresentation
• bait-and-switch
• failure to provide promised services

---

# 5. Bank Liability if They Ignore Evidence

Failure to comply with FCBA creates statutory liability.

15 U.S.C. §1640

Provides damages for violations.

Consumer may recover:

• actual damages
• statutory damages
• attorney fees

Source
[https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/15/1640](https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/15/1640)

---

# 6. Unfair Practices by Banks

Banks can also violate the **Consumer Financial Protection Act (CFPA)**.

12 U.S.C. §5531

This prohibits **unfair or abusive acts in financial services**.

CFPB definition of unfair act:

> An act is unfair when it causes substantial injury to consumers that is not reasonably avoidable.

Source
[https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/12/5531](https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/12/5531)

If a bank:

• knowingly supports fraudulent merchant claims
• denies disputes without investigation
• relies on fabricated agreements

This may constitute a **UDAAP violation**.

---

# 7. Electronic Fund Transfer Act (for debit cards)

If debit was used, the governing law is:

15 U.S.C. §1693f

Requires investigation of unauthorized transfers.

Source
[https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/15/1693f](https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/15/1693f)

---

# 8. Card Network Rules (Visa / Mastercard)

While not statutes, banks **contractually must follow them.**

Example (Visa Core Rules):

Issuers must ensure disputes are supported by evidence and properly reviewed.

Source
[https://usa.visa.com/support/consumer/visa-rules.html](https://usa.visa.com/support/consumer/visa-rules.html)

If a bank ignores evidence, they may violate network rules.

---

# 9. Merchant Contract Enforceability (Important for your case)

If the merchant’s “agreement” was never accepted, **the charge may not be valid.**

Courts consistently hold that online terms require:

• conspicuous notice
• clear assent

If these are missing, **no contract exists**. 

This principle appears in:

• **Specht v. Netscape**
• **Nguyen v. Barnes & Noble**
• **Berman v. Freedom Financial**

All holding that **hidden terms are not binding**. 

---

# 10. When a Bank Becomes Legally Exposed

A bank may face liability if it:

1. ignores evidence
2. relies solely on merchant claims
3. fails to conduct reasonable investigation
4. denies a billing error without explanation

These violate:

• FCBA
• Regulation Z
• CFPA / UDAAP

---

# 11. Evidence That Shows Bank Non-Compliance

Regulators typically look for:

• failure to review submitted evidence
• missing case logs
• unexplained dispute denial
• merchant documents accepted without verification

This is why requesting the **full representment packet and internal logs** is powerful.

---

# Bottom Line

The main laws protecting you are:

| Law                           | What it requires                     |
| ----------------------------- | ------------------------------------ |
| FCBA (15 USC 1666)            | Bank must investigate billing errors |
| Regulation Z (12 CFR 1026.13) | Requires reasonable investigation    |
| CFPA (12 USC 5531)            | Prohibits unfair banking practices   |
| TILA damages (15 USC 1640)    | Allows consumer lawsuits             |
| EFTA (15 USC 1693f)           | Debit card dispute protections       |

A bank **cannot simply side with a merchant** without a real investigation.

---

That rule alone wins a large number of disputes.

# CFPB Complaint – Failure to Conduct Reasonable Billing Error Investigation

**Company:** First Interstate Bank  
**Product:** Credit Card  
**Issue:** Billing Error Investigation Failure

---

# Complaint Narrative

This complaint concerns First Interstate Bank’s handling of a billing-error dispute involving a $10,500 credit-card charge for a “Done-For-You Airbnb business acquisition and deployment program.”

The program was marketed and sold as a fixed-fee service promising the acquisition, setup, and launch of a revenue-generating Airbnb property. However, the promised deliverables were never provided.

Specifically:

• No property was acquired  
• No lease was secured  
• No furnishings were installed  
• No Airbnb listing was launched  
• No bookings occurred  
• No revenue was generated

The dispute therefore concerned **services not delivered as agreed**, which constitutes a billing error under federal law.

Regulation Z defines a billing error as:

> “A reflection on a statement of goods or services not accepted by the consumer or not delivered as agreed.”  
12 CFR §1026.13(a)(3)

Despite extensive documentation showing non-delivery of the core promised service, First Interstate Bank denied the dispute.

---

# Bank Investigation Failure

Federal law requires credit-card issuers to conduct a reasonable investigation when a billing-error notice is received.

Under the **Fair Credit Billing Act (15 U.S.C. §1666)**:

> “If a creditor receives a written notice from the obligor… the creditor shall investigate the matter and correct the billing error.”

Regulation Z further requires:

> “The creditor must conduct a reasonable investigation of the consumer’s claim.”  
12 CFR §1026.13(f)

The CFPB’s official interpretation clarifies that a creditor **cannot deny a billing-error claim solely because the merchant asserts the charge is valid.**

However, the bank’s denial relied primarily on merchant assertions regarding alleged work hours, research performed, and temporary software access rather than addressing the central issue: **the promised service outcome was never delivered.**

The bank therefore treated the merchant’s internal activity claims as sufficient proof of performance, even though the purchased service was a **fixed-fee outcome-based program**, not an hourly consulting arrangement.

---

# Reliance on Disputed Merchant Terms

The merchant’s denial relied on alleged contractual terms and conditions that were never presented to or accepted by the consumer at the time of purchase.

Courts consistently hold that online terms are not enforceable when consumers are not provided reasonably conspicuous notice and do not clearly manifest assent to those terms. fileciteturn0file0

Federal courts have repeatedly rejected attempts to enforce hidden or post-hoc website terms under these circumstances, including:

• **Specht v. Netscape Communications Corp.**  
• **Nguyen v. Barnes & Noble Inc.**  
• **Berman v. Freedom Financial Network, LLC**

These decisions reaffirm the fundamental rule that a contract requires **clear notice and unambiguous assent**. fileciteturn0file0

In this dispute, the merchant relied on unsigned and previously undisclosed terms after payment in order to justify non-delivery of the promised service.

Despite this, First Interstate Bank accepted the merchant’s explanation without independently evaluating whether those alleged terms were ever validly agreed to.

---

# Procedural Closure Based on Alleged Notice

The bank’s denial also relied on an alleged mailed request for additional information dated November 14, 2025.

However, the bank’s investigation was effectively closed based on the absence of a response to this letter, even though the dispute already contained extensive documentation addressing the underlying billing error.

Closing a billing-error investigation based on procedural correspondence rather than evaluating the substance of the evidence raises serious concerns regarding whether a **reasonable investigation** was conducted as required by federal law.

---

# Failure to Provide Investigation Materials

During and after the dispute process, repeated requests were made for the materials the bank relied upon in making its determination, including:

• the merchant representment packet  
• the internal dispute investigation log  
• documentation showing how the bank evaluated the submitted evidence

These materials were not timely produced during the investigation period.

Without access to these records, it is not possible to verify whether the bank conducted a meaningful review of the evidence or simply accepted the merchant’s explanation.

---

# Unfair Banking Practices

Failure to conduct a reasonable billing-error investigation may also constitute an unfair practice under the **Consumer Financial Protection Act (12 U.S.C. §5531)** when it causes substantial financial injury that consumers cannot reasonably avoid.

In this case, the denial of the billing-error dispute resulted in the consumer being held responsible for a $10,500 charge for services that were never delivered as promised.

---

# Requested Resolution

I request that First Interstate Bank:

1. Reopen the billing-error investigation.
2. Conduct a compliant investigation under the Fair Credit Billing Act and Regulation Z.
3. Reverse the disputed $10,500 charge if the merchant cannot substantiate delivery of the promised service.
4. Remove any interest, fees, or adverse credit reporting associated with the disputed charge.
5. Provide the investigation materials relied upon in making the original determination, including the merchant representment packet and internal case log.

---

This complaint **does not repeat your previous filings**.

It focuses specifically on:

• **FCBA investigation duties**  
• **Regulation Z reasonable investigation standard**  
• **CFPB commentary prohibiting blind reliance on merchants**

# CFPB Complaint – Issuer Reliance on Unverified Merchant Contractual Terms

**Company:** First Interstate Bank  
**Product:** Credit Card  
**Issue:** Billing Error Investigation – Regulatory Compliance Review

---

# Complaint Narrative

This complaint concerns First Interstate Bank’s denial of a billing-error dispute involving a $10,500 credit-card charge for a “Done-For-You Airbnb acquisition and deployment program.”

The program was marketed and sold as a fixed-fee service promising the acquisition, setup, and launch of a revenue-generating Airbnb property.

However, the promised deliverables were never provided. No property was acquired, no lease was secured, no Airbnb listing was launched, no bookings occurred, and no revenue was generated.

Under Regulation Z, a billing error includes:

> “A reflection on a statement of goods or services not accepted by the consumer or not delivered as agreed.”  
12 CFR §1026.13(a)(3)

Despite documentation demonstrating that the promised service outcome was never delivered, the bank denied the dispute.

---

# Reliance on Merchant Assertions Instead of Independent Investigation

Federal law requires creditors to conduct a reasonable investigation when a billing error is disputed.

Under the **Fair Credit Billing Act (15 U.S.C. §1666)**, creditors must investigate billing-error notices submitted by consumers.

Regulation Z further provides:

> “The creditor must conduct a reasonable investigation of the consumer’s claim.”  
12 CFR §1026.13(f)

The CFPB’s official interpretation also clarifies that a creditor may not deny a billing-error claim solely because the merchant asserts the charge is valid.

However, the bank’s determination relied heavily on merchant assertions that internal work had been performed (including research and consultation activity) rather than evaluating whether the purchased service—the acquisition and launch of an operational Airbnb property—was actually delivered.

The dispute therefore concerned **non-delivery of the purchased service outcome**, not merely dissatisfaction with the quality or scope of work.

---

# Reliance on Merchant Contractual Terms Without Verifying Contract Formation

The merchant’s rebuttal relied on alleged contractual terms and conditions that were never presented to or accepted by the consumer at the time of purchase.

Courts consistently hold that online contractual terms are enforceable only where users receive reasonably conspicuous notice of those terms and clearly manifest assent to them. fileciteturn0file0

If either element is missing—clear notice or unambiguous agreement—courts generally find that no binding contract exists. fileciteturn0file0

This principle has been reaffirmed in multiple federal cases addressing online terms, including:

• *Specht v. Netscape Communications Corp.*  
• *Nguyen v. Barnes & Noble Inc.*  
• *Berman v. Freedom Financial Network, LLC*

In this dispute, the merchant relied on alleged terms after payment to justify non-delivery of the promised service.

However, there is no evidence that those terms were clearly presented to or accepted by the consumer during the purchase process.

Screenshots from the merchant’s website also show that links to the merchant’s “Terms” and “Privacy Policy” pages return **404 “Page Not Found” errors**, raising additional concerns regarding whether those terms were accessible to consumers at the time of purchase.

Despite this, First Interstate Bank accepted the merchant’s contractual defenses without independently evaluating whether a valid contract containing those terms was ever actually formed.

---

# Concerns Regarding Investigation Methodology

Where a merchant relies on contractual terms to justify non-delivery of services, a reasonable investigation would include evaluating whether those terms were:

• actually presented to the consumer  
• reasonably conspicuous  
• affirmatively accepted during the purchase process

Instead, the bank appears to have treated the merchant’s submission of alleged terms as sufficient proof that those terms governed the transaction.

If those terms were never validly agreed to, the merchant’s contractual defense may be legally invalid, and reliance on those terms could undermine the integrity of the dispute investigation.

---

# Consumer Financial Protection Concerns

Failure to independently evaluate the validity of a merchant’s contractual defenses may also raise concerns under the **Consumer Financial Protection Act (12 U.S.C. §5531)**, which prohibits unfair practices in consumer financial services when consumers suffer substantial financial injury that they cannot reasonably avoid.

In this case, the consumer remains responsible for a $10,500 charge tied to a service that was never delivered as promised.

---

# Requested Resolution

I respectfully request that First Interstate Bank:

1. Reopen the billing-error investigation.
2. Conduct a compliant investigation under the Fair Credit Billing Act and Regulation Z.
3. Evaluate whether the merchant can substantiate delivery of the promised service outcome.
4. Verify whether the merchant’s alleged contractual terms were ever validly presented and accepted.
5. Reverse the disputed $10,500 charge if the merchant cannot demonstrate valid contractual terms and delivery of the promised service.

---

# Why This Complaint Is Distinct From Prior Filings

This complaint focuses specifically on **issuer reliance on unverified merchant contractual terms and the legal validity of those terms**.

It raises concerns regarding whether the bank evaluated the **formation and enforceability of the alleged agreement** before relying on those terms to deny the billing-error dispute.

This issue is separate from the previously raised concerns regarding the underlying service dispute, investigation procedures, and documentation requests.

---

# Key Evidence Supporting This Complaint

Supporting documentation includes:

• merchant marketing materials describing the DFY service  
• invoice and transaction documentation  
• evidence showing no property acquisition or Airbnb listing launch  
• merchant rebuttal referencing alleged terms  
• screenshots showing the merchant’s terms pages returning **404 errors**

---

# Final Note

This complaint seeks regulatory review of whether the bank’s dispute determination complied with federal billing-error investigation requirements and whether the bank relied on merchant contractual defenses without verifying that those terms were legally enforceable.

# What Laws Actually Apply To Your Situation

## 1. **FCBA — 15 U.S.C. §1666**  
✅ **Primary controlling law**

This governs **credit-card billing disputes**.

Key rule:

> “If a creditor receives a written notice of a billing error… the creditor shall investigate the matter and correct the billing error.”  
15 U.S.C. §1666(a)

Source  
https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/15/1666

This statute creates the **bank’s duty to investigate**.

---

## 2. **Regulation Z — 12 CFR §1026.13**  
✅ **Most important regulation**

This is the **implementing rule of the FCBA**.

Key requirement:

> “The creditor must conduct a reasonable investigation of the consumer’s claim.”  
12 CFR §1026.13(f)

Source  
https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1026/13/

CFPB commentary also states:

> A creditor may not deny a claim solely because the merchant asserts the charge is valid.

This is **extremely important** for your case.

---

## 3. **CFPA / UDAAP — 12 U.S.C. §5531**  
✅ **Valid secondary law**

This governs **unfair or abusive banking practices**.

Key principle:

> An act is unfair if it causes substantial injury to consumers that is not reasonably avoidable.

Source  
https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/12/5531

If a bank:

• ignores evidence  
• rubber-stamps merchant claims  
• denies disputes without investigation  

CFPB may treat it as **UDAAP**.

---

# Laws That Should NOT Be Front-and-Center

## 4. **TILA damages — 15 U.S.C. §1640**  
⚠️ **Do not emphasize in CFPB complaint**

This statute creates **civil liability in court**, not CFPB procedure.

It allows recovery of:

• statutory damages  
• attorney fees

But CFPB complaints are **not lawsuits**, so mentioning it heavily can weaken credibility.

You can mention it **once**, lightly.

Source  
https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/15/1640

---

## 5. **EFTA — 15 U.S.C. §1693f**  
❌ **Do not use if this was a credit card**

EFTA applies only to:

• debit cards  
• bank transfers  
• electronic funds transfers

If your transaction was **credit card**, EFTA does not apply.

Source  
https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/15/1693f

---

# Correct Legal Structure For Your CFPB Complaint

Use this hierarchy:

### Primary law
• **FCBA – 15 U.S.C. §1666**

### Implementing regulation
• **Regulation Z – 12 CFR §1026.13**

### Consumer protection oversight
• **CFPA / UDAAP – 12 U.S.C. §5531**

Optional supporting reference:

• **TILA damages – 15 U.S.C. §1640**

---

### Federal Legal Requirements

Under the **Fair Credit Billing Act (15 U.S.C. §1666)**, a creditor who receives written notice of a billing error must investigate the matter and correct the error if one exists.

Regulation Z, which implements the FCBA, further requires:

> “The creditor must conduct a reasonable investigation of the consumer’s claim.”  
12 CFR §1026.13(f)

CFPB commentary clarifies that a creditor may not deny a billing-error claim solely because a merchant asserts the charge is valid.

Where a creditor denies a billing-error claim without independently evaluating the consumer’s evidence, such conduct raises concerns regarding compliance with both the FCBA and Regulation Z.

Failure to conduct a reasonable investigation may also constitute an unfair practice under the **Consumer Financial Protection Act (12 U.S.C. §5531)** when consumers suffer substantial financial injury that they cannot reasonably avoid.

---

# The Most Important Legal Point In Your Case

This rule:

> A creditor may not deny a claim solely because the merchant asserts the charge is valid.

That language comes from the **CFPB Official Interpretation of Regulation Z**.

It is the **single most powerful rule in billing-error disputes**.

CFPB complaints work best when:

• the law is **shortly referenced**

Too many statutes can trigger the **“legal argument / dispute with merchant” filter**.

Use:

✔ CFPA / UDAAP (12 USC 5531)

Do not use:

✖ EFTA (unless debit card)

This complaint concerns First Interstate Bank’s denial of a billing-error dispute based on merchant contractual terms that may not constitute a legally enforceable agreement.
During the investigation of the disputed charge, the bank relied on merchant-submitted “terms and agreement” documents as a primary basis for denying the billing error. However, there is no evidence that those alleged terms were ever clearly presented to or affirmatively accepted by the consumer at the time of the transaction.
There is also documented evidence provided by BOTH the merchant and the cardholder, used and possessed by First Interstate Bank (FIB); showing that the merchant writes in text roughly 1 month after the transaction, that the merchant omitted the terms and agreement at time of purchase.
This has been documented and verified through certified mail, phone, fax, prior dated threads in writing, etc.

Courts consistently hold that online contractual terms are enforceable only where consumers receive reasonably conspicuous notice of the terms and clearly manifest assent to those terms.
If either element is missing - clear notice or unambiguous agreement - courts generally find that no binding contract exists.
This principle has been repeatedly applied by federal courts evaluating website terms and conditions, including, but not limited to Specht v. Netscape Communications Corp., Nguyen v. Barnes & Noble Inc., and Berman v. Freedom Financial Network, LLC., etc.
In this matter, the merchant’s rebuttal relied on alleged contractual terms after payment to justify non-delivery of the promised service. However, there is no evidence that those terms were clearly presented to or unambiguously accepted & agreed to by the consumer during the purchase process. (lack of mutual assent)
Screenshots from the merchant’s website also show that links to the merchant’s “Terms” and “Privacy Policy” pages return 404 “Page Not Found” errors, further solidifying that terms were inaccessible to consumers at the time of purchase.
Despite these issues, First Interstate Bank appears to have accepted the merchant’s non-contractual defenses without independently evaluating whether a valid agreement containing those terms was ever formed; while simultaneously ignoring cardholder’s evidence and statements proving no mutually assenting, and/or signed agreement exists.

Merchant has deceptively created unsigned terms that are significantly and materially different than the documented ads, sales, website, invoice, and material in writing uniformly affirm. This bait and switch tactic is considered deception defined by the FTC, violating FTC Policy, as well as being clearly defined as deceptive acts and practices - that FIB is supporting and enforcing.

Federal Compliance Framework
Federal law establishes clear obligations for credit-card issuers when a consumer disputes a charge.
Under the Fair Credit Billing Act (15 U.S.C. §1666), when a creditor receives written notice of a billing error, the creditor must investigate the matter and correct the error if one exists.
Regulation Z, which implements the Fair Credit Billing Act, further provides:
“The creditor must conduct a reasonable investigation of the consumer’s claim.”
12 CFR §1026.13(f)
Regulation Z also defines a billing error to include:
“A reflection on a statement of goods or services not accepted by the consumer or not delivered as agreed.”
12 CFR §1026.13(a)(3)
The Consumer Financial Protection Bureau’s official interpretation clarifies that a creditor may not deny a billing-error claim solely because a merchant asserts that the charge is valid.
Where a creditor relies on merchant representations or contractual defenses without independently evaluating whether those defenses are legally valid, this raises concerns regarding compliance with the investigation requirements of the Fair Credit Billing Act and Regulation Z.
Failure to conduct a meaningful investigation may also raise concerns under the Consumer Financial Protection Act (12 U.S.C. §5531), which prohibits unfair practices in consumer financial services when consumers suffer substantial financial injury that they cannot reasonably avoid.

Investigation Concerns
In this dispute, the bank appears to have relied on merchant assertions regarding internal work activity and alleged one-way, unsigned, post agreement terms - presented by the merchant months after the chargeback and initial transaction date; rather than evaluating whether the purchased service that the was advertised at the time of purchase was actually delivered as agreed. (mutual assent)
The merchant and the issuer bear the burden of producing substantial evidence to prove that the consumer unambiguously and affirmatively manifested assent to those terms, as the courts and law require.
The disputed charge relates to a fixed-fee service program promising the acquisition, design, furnishing, listing, and launch of an operational profiting Airbnb property. However, the promised service outcome was never delivered.
If the merchant’s alleged post-transaction terms were never validly presented or accepted unambiguously during the purchase process, reliance on those terms to justify denial of the billing-error dispute are not legally binding.
A reasonable investigation would therefore include evaluating whether:
those terms were reasonably conspicuous to the consumer, AND
the consumer unambiguously and affirmatively manifested assent to those terms
The available record raises concerns that the bank accepted the merchant’s contractual defenses without verifying whether those legal prerequisites for contract formation were satisfied.


Request for Regulatory Review
Due to the billing error concerns services not delivered, not agreed to, and the dispute includes evidence contradicting the merchant’s defenses: 
- I respectfully request regulatory review of whether the bank’s investigation satisfied the reasonable-investigation requirement under 12 CFR §1026.13(f) and whether the bank relied on merchant assertions of terms presented months after the transaction, without verifying that those terms were legally enforceable.
Since the dispute concerns goods or services that were not delivered unambiguously agreed, and the record contains evidence contradicting the merchant’s assertions:
I respectfully request review of whether the issuer’s investigation met the reasonable-investigation standard required by 12 CFR §1026.13(f). In particular, I ask that the Bureau evaluate whether the determination relied primarily on merchant representations without independently assessing the consumer’s evidence or verifying the validity of the contractual terms asserted by the merchant.


Requested Resolution
I respectfully request that First Interstate Bank:
Reopen the billing-error investigation.
Conduct a compliant investigation under the Fair Credit Billing Act and Regulation Z.
Evaluate whether the merchant can substantiate both the delivery of the promised service and the existence of a legally valid agreement governing the transaction.
Reverse / reimburse the cardholder the disputed $10,500 charge and all associated interest, with confirmation in writing of the details and numbers in total

# CFPB Complaint – Billing Error Investigation and Reliance on Unverified Merchant Terms

**Company:** First Interstate Bank  
**Product:** Credit Card  
**Issue:** Billing Error Investigation – Reliance on Unverified Contractual Terms

---

# Complaint Narrative

This complaint concerns First Interstate Bank’s denial of a billing-error dispute involving a $10,500 credit-card charge for a “Done-For-You Airbnb business acquisition and deployment program.”

The bank’s determination appears to rely on merchant-submitted contractual terms that may not constitute a legally enforceable agreement.

During the investigation of the disputed charge, First Interstate Bank relied on merchant-submitted “terms and agreement” documents as a primary basis for denying the billing error. However, there is no evidence that those alleged terms were clearly presented to or affirmatively accepted by the consumer at the time of the transaction.

In fact, documented communications from the merchant approximately one month after the transaction confirm that the merchant **omitted the terms and agreement at the time of purchase.**

This evidence has been provided to the bank through multiple channels, including written communications, certified mail, fax, and supporting documentation submitted during the dispute process.

---

# Legal Context – Contract Formation and Online Terms

Courts consistently hold that online contractual terms are enforceable only where consumers receive **reasonably conspicuous notice of the terms and clearly manifest assent to those terms.** fileciteturn0file0

If either element is missing—clear notice or unambiguous agreement—courts generally find that **no binding contract exists.** fileciteturn0file0

This principle has been repeatedly applied by federal courts evaluating website terms and conditions, including:

• *Specht v. Netscape Communications Corp.*  
• *Nguyen v. Barnes & Noble Inc.*  
• *Berman v. Freedom Financial Network, LLC*

In this matter, the merchant’s rebuttal relied on alleged contractual terms after payment to justify non-delivery of the promised service.

However:

- there is no evidence that those terms were clearly presented during the purchase process  
- there is no evidence that the consumer unambiguously manifested assent to those terms  
- the merchant has acknowledged that the terms were omitted at the time of purchase

Screenshots from the merchant’s website also show that links to the merchant’s **Terms** and **Privacy Policy** pages return **404 “Page Not Found” errors**, further raising concerns regarding whether those terms were accessible to consumers.

Despite this, First Interstate Bank appears to have accepted the merchant’s contractual defenses without independently evaluating whether a valid agreement containing those terms was ever formed.

---

# Federal Compliance Framework

Federal law establishes clear obligations for credit-card issuers when a consumer disputes a charge.

### Fair Credit Billing Act – 15 U.S.C. §1666

Under the **Fair Credit Billing Act**, when a creditor receives written notice of a billing error, the creditor must investigate the matter and correct the error if one exists.

This statute governs credit-card billing disputes and imposes a duty on issuers to conduct a proper investigation.

---

### Regulation Z – 12 CFR §1026.13

Regulation Z, which implements the Fair Credit Billing Act, further requires:

> “The creditor must conduct a reasonable investigation of the consumer’s claim.”  
12 CFR §1026.13(f)

Regulation Z also defines a billing error to include:

> “A reflection on a statement of goods or services not accepted by the consumer or not delivered as agreed.”  
12 CFR §1026.13(a)(3)

The Consumer Financial Protection Bureau’s official interpretation clarifies that a creditor **may not deny a billing-error claim solely because the merchant asserts that the charge is valid.**

---

### Consumer Financial Protection Act – 12 U.S.C. §5531

The **Consumer Financial Protection Act** prohibits unfair, deceptive, or abusive acts and practices in consumer financial services.

An act may be considered **unfair** when it causes substantial financial injury to consumers that they cannot reasonably avoid.

If a creditor denies a billing-error claim without conducting a meaningful investigation or relies on merchant assertions that contradict available evidence, such conduct may raise concerns under this statute.

---

### Truth in Lending Act Liability – 15 U.S.C. §1640

Failure to comply with obligations under the Truth in Lending Act and the Fair Credit Billing Act may also create civil liability, including statutory damages and attorney’s fees.

---

# Investigation Concerns

The bank’s investigation appears to rely primarily on merchant assertions regarding internal work activity and alleged contractual terms that were presented months after the transaction.

However, the purchased service was a **fixed-fee program promising the acquisition, setup, design, furnishing, listing, and launch of an operational Airbnb property capable of generating revenue.**

The promised service outcome was never delivered.

No property was acquired.  
No lease was secured.  
No Airbnb listing was launched.  
No bookings occurred.  
No revenue was generated.

If the merchant’s alleged terms were never clearly presented or accepted during the purchase process, reliance on those terms to deny the billing-error dispute may effectively validate contractual provisions that were never legally binding.

A reasonable investigation would therefore include evaluating whether:

• the alleged contractual terms were reasonably conspicuous to the consumer  
• the consumer affirmatively manifested assent to those terms  
• the merchant can substantiate delivery of the promised service

The available record raises concerns that the bank accepted the merchant’s contractual defenses without verifying whether those legal prerequisites for contract formation were satisfied.

---

# Concerns Regarding Merchant Misrepresentation

The merchant has also created unsigned terms that materially contradict the documented advertisements, sales representations, website content, invoice, and written materials describing the service.

This appears consistent with a **bait-and-switch tactic**, where the terms governing the transaction are altered after payment in order to justify non-delivery of the promised service.

Such conduct may constitute deceptive practices under established consumer protection standards.

If the bank relied on these altered or post-transaction terms without verifying their validity, this further raises concerns regarding whether the dispute investigation satisfied the reasonable-investigation requirements under federal law.

---

# Request for Regulatory Review

Because this dispute concerns services not delivered as agreed and includes evidence contradicting the merchant’s contractual defenses, I respectfully request regulatory review of whether the bank’s investigation satisfied the **reasonable-investigation requirement under 12 CFR §1026.13(f)**.

In particular, I ask that the Bureau evaluate whether the issuer’s determination relied primarily on merchant representations and post-transaction contractual terms rather than independently assessing the evidence submitted by the consumer and verifying whether those contractual terms were legally enforceable.

---

# Requested Resolution

I respectfully request that First Interstate Bank:

1. Reopen the billing-error investigation.
2. Conduct a compliant investigation under the Fair Credit Billing Act and Regulation Z.
3. Evaluate whether the merchant can substantiate both the delivery of the promised service and the existence of a legally valid agreement governing the transaction.
4. Reverse and reimburse the disputed $10,500 charge and all associated interest or fees.
5. Provide written confirmation of the corrected account balance.

## 🤖 ChatGPT

To make the **agreement issue crystal clear**, focus on three **separate legal questions**. Right now the bank is collapsing them into one. Your job is to **separate them explicitly**.

---

# 1. The Three Separate Questions

### Question 1 — Was there a valid agreement to the merchant’s terms?

For the merchant’s terms to govern the transaction, the bank must be able to show:

1. **Conspicuous notice of the terms**
2. **Unambiguous manifestation of assent**

Courts repeatedly hold that both are required for online terms to be binding. fileciteturn0file0

If either is missing, **no binding agreement exists**. fileciteturn0file0

Key point for your case:

The merchant **admitted the terms were omitted at the time of purchase**, which directly undermines both elements.

---

### Question 2 — Did the consumer agree to new terms later?

The bank appears to be arguing:

> “The consumer kept working with the merchant after the refund/downscale, so they accepted the new terms.”

That argument only works if **both of these are true**:

1. The new terms were **clearly presented**
2. The consumer **clearly agreed to them**

Continuing communication or attempting to resolve a problem **does not automatically create contractual acceptance**.

Courts consistently require **clear and affirmative agreement**, not implied agreement from conduct alone. fileciteturn0file0

---

### Question 3 — Was the purchased service delivered?

Even if a valid agreement existed, the bank must still determine whether:

> the goods or services were **delivered as agreed**

Regulation Z defines a billing error as:

> “A reflection on a statement of goods or services not accepted by the consumer or not delivered as agreed.”  
12 CFR §1026.13(a)(3)

So the investigation must answer:

- What service was **advertised and sold**
- What service was **actually delivered**

---

# 2. The Logical Structure You Want the Regulator to See

Spell it out simply.

### Step 1
Merchant admits **terms were omitted at purchase**.

→ No mutual assent to those terms.

### Step 2
Merchant later introduces **new terms and refund conditions**.

→ These require **new agreement**.

### Step 3
No evidence of **express acceptance** of those new terms.

→ No binding modification of the agreement.

### Step 4
Service outcome promised in the transaction was **never delivered**.

→ Billing error under Regulation Z.

---

# 3. The Exact Sentence That Clarifies This

Add this to your letter:

> The bank’s determination appears to treat continued communication with the merchant as acceptance of newly asserted contractual terms. However, contract law requires a clear and unambiguous manifestation of assent before such terms become binding. Where the merchant acknowledges that the terms were omitted at the time of purchase and there is no evidence that the consumer affirmatively agreed to those terms later, reliance on those terms to deny the billing-error dispute raises serious concerns regarding the validity of the agreement itself.

---

# 4. Why the Bank Is Making This Argument

Banks often rely on a concept called **“course of conduct.”**

They argue:

> The consumer kept engaging with the merchant → therefore they accepted the arrangement.

But this **does not override the requirement of assent** when new contractual terms are introduced.

That’s why your **legal context document is important**.

Courts repeatedly emphasize:

- notice
- assent
- mutual agreement

as prerequisites for enforceable terms. fileciteturn0file0

---

# 5. The One Question That Forces the Issue

In your complaint or letter, frame the issue like this:

> What evidence demonstrates that the consumer was provided clear notice of the merchant’s alleged terms and unambiguously manifested assent to those terms before they were relied upon to deny the billing-error dispute?

This question forces the bank to identify **actual proof of agreement**.

---

# 6. What Evidence Actually Proves Agreement

Examples that would prove assent:

- signed contract  
- checkbox acceptance  
- recorded statement agreeing to terms  
- written acknowledgement of the terms

If the bank **cannot produce one of these**, their reliance on the terms becomes questionable.

---

# 7. The Core Point You Want the CFPB to Focus On

Your complaint is **not simply about a bad merchant**.

It is about whether the bank:

- relied on **terms that may never have been agreed to**
- used those terms to **deny a billing error**
- without verifying **contract formation**

That is a **bank investigation issue**, which falls under:

- **FCBA – 15 USC 1666**
- **Regulation Z – 12 CFR 1026.13**
